🌊 Indian Geography: The Himalayan River Systems
Which is the longest river in India? The Ganga (2,525 km).

The Ganga originates from the Gangotri glacier under what name? Bhagirathi.

At which exact location do the Bhagirathi and Alaknanda rivers merge to officially form the Ganga? Devprayag (Uttarakhand).

Which is the longest and largest right-bank tributary of the Ganga? The Yamuna.

Where does the Yamuna River join the Ganga? Prayagraj (Triveni Sangam).

Which major river originates near the Mansarovar Lake in Tibet and is known locally as the "Tsangpo"? The Brahmaputra.

Through which Indian state does the Brahmaputra enter the country? Arunachal Pradesh (where it is known as the Dihang or Siang).

Majuli, the world's largest river island, is formed by which river? The Brahmaputra (in Assam).

Which river is known as the "Sorrow of Bihar" due to its frequent course changes and flooding? The Kosi River.

Which river was historically known as the "Sorrow of Bengal"? The Damodar River.

Where does the Indus River originate? Near Mansarovar Lake (Bokhar Chu glacier in Tibet).

Name the five major tributaries of the Indus (the "Panchnad") that flow through Punjab. Jhelum, Chenab, Ravi, Beas, and Sutlej.

Which tributary of the Indus originates from the Beas Kund in Himachal Pradesh? The Beas River.

Which major river flows directly through the Wular Lake in Jammu & Kashmir? The Jhelum River.

Which major peninsular river originates from the Amarkantak plateau and flows north to join the Ganga? The Son River.

🚤 Indian Geography: Peninsular Rivers & Lakes
Which is the longest river in Peninsular India? The Godavari.

Which river is respectfully referred to as the "Dakshin Ganga" (Ganges of the South)? The Godavari.

Where does the Godavari River originate? Trimbakeshwar (Nashik, Maharashtra).

Name the two largest west-flowing peninsular rivers that drain into the Arabian Sea. Narmada and Tapi (Tapti).

Which river uniquely flows through a rift valley between the Vindhya and Satpura mountain ranges? The Narmada River.

Where does the Narmada River originate? The Amarkantak Plateau (Madhya Pradesh).

The spectacular Dhuandhar Falls (Marble Rocks) are located on which river? The Narmada River (near Jabalpur).

Where does the Krishna River originate? Mahabaleshwar (Maharashtra).

The city of Hyderabad is situated on the banks of which river? The Musi River (a tributary of the Krishna).

Where does the Kaveri (Cauvery) River originate? Talakaveri in the Brahmagiri Hills (Karnataka).

Which river is commonly known as the "Ganga of the South" due to its immense sacredness in South India? The Kaveri River.

The Hirakud Dam, the longest earthen dam in India, is built across which river? The Mahanadi River (in Odisha).

Which Indian river famously crosses the Tropic of Cancer twice? The Mahi River.

Which river, known as the "Lifeline of Goa", flows into the Arabian Sea? The Mandovi River.

Which river forms an inland drainage system, disappearing into the Rann of Kutch? The Luni River.

Which is the largest freshwater lake in India? Wular Lake (Jammu & Kashmir).

Which is the largest brackish water lagoon in India? Chilika Lake (Odisha).

The famous Keibul Lamjao National Park floats on which lake? Loktak Lake (Manipur).

Which lake in Maharashtra was created by a massive meteorite impact? Lonar Lake.

Which is the longest lake in India? Vembanad Lake (Kerala).

🌤️ Indian Geography: Climate & The Monsoon
What is the primary, overarching climate classification of India? Tropical Monsoon Climate.

Which seasonal wind system is responsible for over 70% of India's annual rainfall? The Southwest Monsoon.

The Southwest Monsoon splits into two distinct branches over India. What are they? The Arabian Sea branch and the Bay of Bengal branch.

Which Indian state is typically the first to receive the Southwest Monsoon showers (usually by June 1st)? Kerala.

Which place in India records the highest average annual rainfall in the world? Mawsynram (Meghalaya).

Which coastal region of India remains relatively dry during the Southwest Monsoon but receives heavy rainfall during the winter (Retreating Monsoon)? The Coromandel Coast (Tamil Nadu).

The vital winter rainfall in Northwestern India, which benefits the Rabi crops (like wheat), is brought by what weather phenomenon? Western Disturbances.

Where do these Western Disturbances originate before traveling to India? The Mediterranean Sea.

What are the pre-monsoon showers in Karnataka and Kerala, which help in the early ripening of fruit, commonly called? Mango Showers.

What are the violent, destructive pre-monsoon thunderstorms in West Bengal and Assam called? Kal Baisakhi (Nor'westers).

In Assam, the Kal Baisakhi thunderstorms are locally known as what? Bardoli Chheerha.

What is the meteorological term for the period of hot, oppressive weather in October caused by the retreating monsoon and high humidity? October Heat.

Which global oceanic phenomenon generally suppresses the Indian monsoon, leading to drought-like conditions? El Niño.

Which global oceanic phenomenon generally enhances the Indian monsoon, leading to excess rainfall? La Niña.

Which hot, dry, and suffocating wind blows over the plains of North and Northwest India during summer? The Loo.

🌱 Indian Geography: Soils & Agriculture
Which is the most widely distributed and fertile soil group in India, covering about 40% of the land area? Alluvial Soil.

Indian soil types map, AI generated
Shutterstock
In the context of alluvial soil, what is the older, less fertile, and more clayey soil called? Bhangar.

What is the newer, highly fertile alluvial soil found in the floodplains called? Khadar.

Which soil type is predominantly found in the Deccan Plateau region? Black Soil.

Black soil is exceptionally suited for cultivating which cash crop? Cotton (hence called Black Cotton Soil).

What is the local Indian name for Black Soil? Regur Soil.

Which rock is the primary parent material for the formation of Black Soil? Basalt (volcanic rock).

Which soil develops a reddish color due to a wide diffusion of iron in crystalline and metamorphic rocks? Red Soil.

Which soil is formed under conditions of high temperature and heavy rainfall, characterized by intense leaching? Laterite Soil.

Laterite soil is poor in organic matter but excellent for cultivating which plantation crops? Cashew nuts, tea, coffee, and tapioca.

What is the process called where heavy rainfall washes down the essential nutrients from the topsoil? Leaching.

Arid (Desert) soils are characteristically high in what chemical component, making them saline? Soluble salts.

Peaty and marshy soils are primarily found in regions with what environmental conditions? Heavy rainfall and high humidity (waterlogged, organic-rich areas).

Which type of severe soil erosion is highly prevalent in the Chambal Valley, forming a rugged "badland" topography? Gully erosion.

What is the agricultural practice of planting lines of trees to reduce wind speed and protect the soil called? Shelterbelts.

Which Indian state is the largest producer of wheat? Uttar Pradesh.

Which Indian state is the largest producer of rice? West Bengal.

Which state is the undisputed largest producer of sugarcane? Uttar Pradesh.

Which state is the largest producer of cotton? Gujarat.

Which state is the largest producer of jute? West Bengal.

Which state is the largest producer of tea? Assam.

Which state is the largest producer of coffee? Karnataka.

Which state is the largest producer of natural rubber? Kerala.

Which state is the largest producer of groundnuts? Gujarat.

Which state or union territory is the largest producer of saffron in India? Jammu and Kashmir.

🌳 Indian Geography: Natural Vegetation & Forests
According to the National Forest Policy of India, what is the ideal target percentage of geographical area that should be under forest cover? 33%.

According to the latest India State of Forest Report (ISFR), which state has the largest total area under forest cover? Madhya Pradesh.

Which Indian state has the highest percentage of its geographical area under forest cover? Mizoram.

Which is the most widespread type of forest vegetation in India? Tropical Deciduous Forests (also known as Monsoon Forests).

During which season do Tropical Deciduous Forests characteristically shed their leaves? Dry summer (for about 6 to 8 weeks).

Teak, Sal, Shisham, and Sandalwood are prominent, commercially valuable tree species of which forest type? Tropical Deciduous Forests.

Mahogany, Ebony, and Rosewood are characteristic trees of which highly dense forest type? Tropical Evergreen Forests.

Which type of specialized vegetation is found in the Sundarbans delta? Mangrove Forests (Tidal Forests).

What is the name of the most prominent, salt-tolerant tree species found in the Sundarbans? Sundari tree.

Pine, Deodar, and Spruce are cone-shaped trees characteristic of which forest type found in the Himalayas? Montane Coniferous Forests.

In which specific biosphere reserve and mountain range are the famous temperate "Shola" forests found? The Nilgiri Hills (Western Ghats).

Which state is renowned for possessing the largest total area of mangrove forests in India? West Bengal.

Which state has the second-largest area of mangrove forests in India? Gujarat.

Which state is known for having the maximum number of officially recognized National Parks in India? Madhya Pradesh.

Where is the headquarters of the Forest Survey of India (FSI), the organization that publishes the forest reports? Dehradun (Uttarakhand).

Where is the Indian Institute of Forest Management (IIFM) situated? Bhopal (Madhya Pradesh).

Where is the Arid Forest Research Institute located? Jodhpur (Rajasthan).

What is the name of the extensive wildlife conservation initiative launched in 1973 to protect the Bengal Tiger? Project Tiger.

In which year was "Project Elephant" launched by the Government of India? 1992.

Which is the largest Tiger Reserve in India by total area? Nagarjunsagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve (Andhra Pradesh/Telangana).

Which National Park in Assam is globally famous for hosting two-thirds of the world's great one-horned rhinoceroses? Kaziranga National Park.

Which National Park in Gujarat is the sole home of the Asiatic Lion? Gir National Park.

The Dachigam National Park in Jammu & Kashmir is famous for the conservation of which critically endangered animal? The Hangul (Kashmir Stag).

Which biosphere reserve was the very first to be established in India (in 1986)? The Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve.

The Gulf of Mannar Biosphere Reserve is particularly famous for the conservation of which marine mammal? The Dugong (Sea Cow).

⛏️ Indian Geography: Minerals & Energy Resources
Which state in India has the largest proven reserves of coal? Jharkhand.

Which state is currently the largest producer of coal in India? Chhattisgarh (followed closely by Odisha).

Over 98% of India's coal reserves belong to which geological rock system? The Gondwana system.

Which is the oldest coalfield in India, located in West Bengal? Raniganj.

Which is the highest quality (carbon content) of coal, found only in small quantities in Jammu & Kashmir? Anthracite.

Which is the most common and widely used type of coal found in India? Bituminous.

Neyveli in Tamil Nadu is famous for mining which type of low-grade, brownish coal? Lignite.

Which state is the largest producer of crude petroleum in India (onshore)? Rajasthan.

Which is the largest and most productive offshore oilfield in India? Bombay High (Mumbai High).

Which is the oldest, first-discovered oil well in India? Digboi (Assam).

Ankleshwar and Kalol are prominent oilfields located in which state? Gujarat.

Which state holds the largest reserves and is the largest producer of Iron Ore (Hematite) in India? Odisha.

The famous Bailadila mines, known for high-grade iron ore, are located in which state? Chhattisgarh.

The Kudremukh iron ore deposits, entirely dedicated to export, are located in which state? Karnataka.

Which is the highest grade of iron ore (containing up to 72% iron)? Magnetite.

Which Indian state is the undisputed largest producer of Bauxite (the primary ore of aluminum)? Odisha.

Which state has a near-monopoly on the production of Mica in India? Andhra Pradesh (Nellore district).

The Khetri copper belt, historically famous since the Indus Valley Civilization, is in which state? Rajasthan.

Where are the famous Kolar Gold Fields (KGF) and Hutti Gold Mines located? Karnataka.

Which state is famous for the Panna diamond mines? Madhya Pradesh.

The Jaduguda mine in Jharkhand is famous for the extraction of which radioactive mineral? Uranium.

The monazite sands of the Kerala coast are the world's largest source of which radioactive element? Thorium.

Which state is the largest producer of Manganese ore in India? Madhya Pradesh.

Where is the largest solar power plant in India (Bhadla Solar Park) located? Rajasthan.

Where is the largest wind farm in India (Muppandal Wind Farm) located? Tamil Nadu.

Which was the first nuclear power station commissioned in India (in 1969)? Tarapur Atomic Power Station (Maharashtra).

The Kudankulam Nuclear Power Plant, built with Russian collaboration, is in which state? Tamil Nadu.

The Kakrapar Atomic Power Station is located in which state? Gujarat.

Which valley is known as the "Ruhr of India" due to its rich coal and mineral deposits? The Damodar Valley.

Which region is known as the "Mineral Heartland of India"? The Chota Nagpur Plateau.

🚆 Indian Geography: Transport & Communications
In which year was the first passenger railway line opened in India (between Bombay and Thane)? 1853.

Currently, the Indian Railways network is divided into how many operational zones? 19 zones (including the Metro Railway, Kolkata, and the South Coast Railway).

Which is the longest railway route in India, running from Dibrugarh (Assam) to Kanyakumari (Tamil Nadu)? The Vivek Express route.

Which railway project represents an engineering marvel, running along the western coast parallel to the Arabian Sea? The Konkan Railway (connecting Roha in Maharashtra to Mangaluru in Karnataka).

Where is the headquarters of the Konkan Railway Corporation located? Navi Mumbai.

Which is the longest National Highway in India? NH-44 (Srinagar to Kanyakumari).

The massive "Golden Quadrilateral" highway project connects which four major metropolitan cities? Delhi, Mumbai, Chennai, and Kolkata.

The North-South Corridor (Srinagar to Kanyakumari) and the East-West Corridor (Porbandar to Silchar) intersect at which city? Jhansi (Uttar Pradesh).

Which is the longest National Waterway in India (NW-1)? The Ganga-Bhagirathi-Hooghly river system (from Prayagraj to Haldia).

National Waterway 2 (NW-2) is located on which river? The Brahmaputra River (from Sadiya to Dhubri in Assam).

How many "Major Ports" (administered by the Central Government) are there on the Indian coastline? 12 (or 13 if Port Blair is counted, though its status varies; 12 mainland major ports).

Which is the largest natural seaport in India by volume of cargo handled? Mumbai Port.

Which port was built specifically to relieve the congestion at the Mumbai Port and is India's largest container port? Jawaharlal Nehru Port Trust (JNPT / Nhava Sheva).

Which major port in Gujarat is a tidal port, built to compensate for the loss of Karachi port after partition? Kandla Port (Deendayal Port).

Which is the oldest artificial port on the eastern coast of India? Chennai Port.

Which is the deepest landlocked and protected port in India? Visakhapatnam Port (Andhra Pradesh).

Which port is situated on the mouth of the Zuari River and is the premier iron-ore exporting port? Mormugao Port (Goa).

Which port is known as the "Queen of the Arabian Sea"? Kochi (Cochin) Port.

Which port in Odisha was developed primarily to export iron ore to Japan? Paradip Port.

Which is the only riverine major port in India? Kolkata Port (Syama Prasad Mookerjee Port).

🌍 World Geography: Human, Economic & Tribes
What is the traditional ice dwelling of the Inuit (Eskimo) people of the Arctic regions called? Igloo.

Which tribal group, known for their short stature, primarily inhabits the dense equatorial forests of the Congo Basin in Africa? The Pygmies.

The Bedouins are a traditionally nomadic Arab ethnic group primarily found in which desert? The Arabian Desert.

The Bushmen (San people) are indigenous hunter-gatherers of which African desert? The Kalahari Desert.

The Maasai people, famous for their red garments and jumping dances, are semi-nomadic pastoralists in which region? East Africa (Kenya and Tanzania).

The indigenous Maori people are native to which country? New Zealand.

The Aborigines are the indigenous people of which continent/country? Australia.

What is the local name for shifting agriculture (slash-and-burn) practiced in the northeastern states of India? Jhuming.

What is the local name for shifting agriculture practiced in Central America and Mexico? Milpa.

What is the local name for shifting agriculture practiced in Brazil? Roca.

What is the local name for shifting agriculture practiced in Indonesia and Malaysia? Ladang.

Which agricultural practice involves growing crops and raising livestock simultaneously on the same farm? Mixed Farming.

Viticulture is the specialized cultivation of what? Grapes (primarily for winemaking).

Pisciculture is the controlled breeding and rearing of what? Fish.

Sericulture involves the rearing of what for commercial production? Silkworms (for silk).

Which region in Germany is historically famous for being Europe's largest industrial and coal-mining area? The Ruhr Valley.

Which region in the United States is globally famous as the hub for high technology and innovation? Silicon Valley (California).

Detroit, USA, is historically famous as the global center for which industry? The Automobile Industry.

Which is the largest and most highly industrialized region (plain) in Japan? The Kanto Plain.

Which country is currently the world's largest producer and consumer of coal? China.

Which country holds the world's largest proven oil reserves? Venezuela.

Which country is the world's largest producer of natural gas? United States (followed closely by Russia).

Which country is the world's largest producer of uranium? Kazakhstan.

Which country is known as the "Sugar Bowl of the World"? Cuba (historically) or Brazil (currently the largest producer).

Which country is the world's largest producer of coffee? Brazil.

📊 Indian Geography: Demographics & Census 2011
According to the 2011 Census, what was the total population of India? 1.21 Billion (1,210 million).

Which Indian state has the highest total population? Uttar Pradesh.

Which Indian state has the lowest total population? Sikkim.

Which Union Territory has the highest population? Delhi.

Which Union Territory has the lowest population? Lakshadweep.

What is the population density of India (persons per sq. km) as per the 2011 Census? 382 persons per sq. km.

Which Indian state has the highest population density? Bihar (1,106 persons per sq. km).

Which Indian state has the lowest population density? Arunachal Pradesh (17 persons per sq. km).

What was the overall sex ratio (females per 1000 males) of India in 2011? 943.

Which Indian state has the highest sex ratio? Kerala (1,084).

Which Indian state has the lowest sex ratio? Haryana (879).

What was the overall literacy rate of India in 2011? 74.04%.

Which state has the highest literacy rate in India? Kerala (94%).

Which state has the lowest literacy rate in India? Bihar (61.8%).

Which Union Territory has the highest literacy rate? Lakshadweep (91.8%).

What percentage of India's population lived in urban areas in 2011? 31.16%.

Which state is the most urbanized in India (highest percentage of urban population)? Goa.

Which state has the highest absolute number of people living in urban areas? Maharashtra.

Which state is the least urbanized (highest percentage of rural population)? Himachal Pradesh.

According to the 2011 Census, which is the most populous city (urban agglomeration) in India? Mumbai.

Which schedule of the Indian Constitution officially lists the 22 recognized languages? The Eighth Schedule.

Which language is the most widely spoken in India? Hindi.

Which is the second most widely spoken language in India? Bengali.

Which state has the highest percentage of Scheduled Tribes (STs) relative to its total population? Mizoram.

Which state has the largest absolute population of Scheduled Tribes (STs)? Madhya Pradesh.

🌪️ Advanced Climatology & Oceanography
What is the global atmospheric circulation cell located between the equator and 30° latitude called? The Hadley Cell.

global atmospheric circulation cells, AI generated
Shutterstock
What is the circulation cell located between 30° and 60° latitude called? The Ferrel Cell.

What is the equatorial low-pressure belt, characterized by calm winds and sudden heavy showers, commonly known as? The Doldrums.

What are the subtropical high-pressure belts (around 30° to 35° latitude), historically known for calm winds that delayed sailing ships, called? The Horse Latitudes.

Which scale is used to measure the intensity of a tornado based on the damage it inflicts? The Fujita Scale.

On a weather map, what is an "Isohyet"? A line joining places that receive equal amounts of rainfall.

What is an "Isohaline"? A line joining points in the ocean with equal salinity.

What is an "Isobath"? A line connecting points of equal underwater depth.

Which oceanic region is known as the 'Herring Pond' due to its massive commercial fishing grounds? The Atlantic Ocean.

The Sargasso Sea, famous for its distinct brown Sargassum seaweed and having no land boundaries, is located in which ocean? The North Atlantic Ocean.

What is the process called where a water body becomes overly enriched with nutrients, leading to dense plant growth and oxygen depletion? Eutrophication.

What are deep, V-shaped valleys cut into the continental slope and edge of the continental shelf called? Submarine Canyons.

Which is the largest and most powerful ocean current in the world, flowing clockwise around Antarctica? The Antarctic Circumpolar Current.

What global weather phenomenon is triggered by the unusual warming of surface waters in the eastern Pacific Ocean? El Niño.

What is the primary cause of modern "ocean acidification"? The absorption of excess carbon dioxide (CO 
2
​
 ) from the atmosphere.

⛰️ Geomorphology & World Borders
What is a "Caldera"? A large volcanic crater formed by a major eruption leading to the collapse of the volcano's mouth.

What is an "Oxbow Lake"? A U-shaped lake that forms when a wide meander of a river is cut off from the main channel.

The spectacular Grand Canyon in the USA was carved over millions of years by which river? The Colorado River.

What is a "Fjord"? A long, narrow, deep inlet of the sea between high cliffs, typically formed by the submergence of a glaciated valley (common in Norway).

What is an "Estuary"? The tidal mouth of a large river, where fresh water from the river meets the saltwater of the ocean.

Which two major Indian rivers form estuaries instead of deltas before draining into the Arabian Sea? The Narmada and Tapti rivers.

What is the geographical term for a block of land bounded by normal faults on either side that has been uplifted? A Horst (or Block Mountain).

The Vindhyas and Satpuras in India are examples of what type of mountains? Block Mountains.

The 49th Parallel forms the international boundary between which two countries? The United States and Canada.

The 38th Parallel acts as the highly militarized dividing line between which two nations? North Korea and South Korea.

Which fortified defensive boundary was built by France in the 1930s to deter an invasion by Germany? The Maginot Line.

Which canal connects the Mediterranean Sea to the Red Sea? The Suez Canal.

Which water body separates the Andaman Islands from the Nicobar Islands? The Ten Degree Channel.

Which maritime channel separates the Lakshadweep Islands from the Maldives? The Eight Degree Channel.

Which strait separates India from Sri Lanka? The Palk Strait.

💧 Indian Agriculture, Irrigation & Projects
The Bhakra Nangal Dam, one of the highest gravity dams in the world, is built on which river? The Sutlej River.

The Hirakud Dam, the longest earthen dam in the world, is located on which river? The Mahanadi River.

The Sardar Sarovar Dam is built across which river? The Narmada River.

The Tehri Dam, the highest dam in India, is built at the confluence of which two rivers? The Bhagirathi and Bhilangana rivers.

The Nagarjuna Sagar Dam is situated on which river? The Krishna River.

The historic Mettur Dam is built across which river? The Kaveri River.

What is the name of the longest irrigation canal in India, which brings water to the Thar Desert? The Indira Gandhi Canal.

Which Indian state has the highest percentage of its net sown area under irrigation? Punjab.

Tank irrigation is most heavily prevalent in which geographical region of India? Peninsular India (the South).

Which Indian state is the undisputed largest producer of natural rubber? Kerala.

Which state is the leading producer of coffee in India? Karnataka.

Which state is the largest producer of tea? Assam.

Which state leads India in the production of cotton? Gujarat.

In the context of Indian agriculture, "Golden Fiber" refers to which crop? Jute.

Which state is the largest producer of jute in India? West Bengal.

The "Yellow Revolution" in India was aimed at increasing the production of what? Oilseeds.

The "Blue Revolution" is related to the commercial production of what? Fish and Aquaculture.

The "White Revolution" (Operation Flood) made India the world's largest producer of what? Milk.

What is the traditional "slash and burn" shifting agriculture known as in Northeast India? Jhum cultivation.

What is the cultivation of grapes specifically for winemaking called? Viticulture.

Which national park in Gujarat is the only remaining natural habitat of the Asiatic Lion? Gir National Park.

Keibul Lamjao, the only floating national park in the world, is located in which state? Manipur.

🧑‍🤝‍🧑 Human Geography & Global Facts
What is the demographic term for the movement of people from rural areas to urban cities? Urbanization.

Among major countries, which nation has the highest population density in the world? Bangladesh.

Which is the most spoken language in the world by the total number of speakers (native + non-native)? English (followed closely by Mandarin Chinese).

What is the demographic term for the number of live births per thousand people in a population per year? Crude Birth Rate.

What is the economic growth potential that results from a shift in a population's age structure (having more working-age people than dependents) called? Demographic Dividend.

Which is the largest landlocked country in the world? Kazakhstan.

Which country is famously known as the "Land of the Midnight Sun"? Norway.

Which country is historically known as the "Sugar Bowl of the World"? Cuba.

🪐 Space, Earth Mechanics & Final Review
What is the outermost layer of the Sun's atmosphere called, which is visible during a total solar eclipse? The Corona.

What are the darker, cooler, magnetically active patches on the Sun's surface called? Sunspots.

Which planet currently has the highest number of confirmed moons in our solar system? Saturn.

What is the vast belt of rocky debris located between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter called? The Asteroid Belt.

What is the region beyond Neptune populated by icy bodies and dwarf planets (like Pluto) called? The Kuiper Belt.

What is the exact mathematical shape of the Earth's orbit around the Sun? Elliptical.

When the Moon passes exactly between the Earth and the Sun, casting a shadow on Earth, what event occurs? A Solar Eclipse.

solar eclipse geometry, AI generated
Getty Images
Explore
When the Earth passes exactly between the Sun and the Moon, casting a shadow on the Moon, what event occurs? A Lunar Eclipse.

What is the term for the day of the year when the Earth is closest to the Sun (usually around January 3rd)? Perihelion.

What is the term for the day of the year when the Earth is farthest from the Sun (usually around July 4th)? Aphelion.

What was the name of the single supercontinent that existed approximately 335 million years ago? Pangaea.

What was the name of the global superocean that surrounded Pangaea? Panthalassa.

The Indian tectonic plate is continuously moving in which general direction? North / North-East.

Which major mountain range is currently forming and rising as a direct result of the collision between the Indian Plate and the Eurasian Plate? The Himalayas.

Which type of volcano has very gentle slopes and is composed largely of highly fluid basaltic lava flows? A Shield Volcano (e.g., Mauna Loa).

The Hawaiian Islands are an example of volcanic activity occurring over what specific geological feature? A Mantle Hotspot.

What is the meteorological term for a prolonged period of abnormally low rainfall, leading to a severe shortage of water? Drought.

What is the upper surface or level of groundwater saturated in the soil or rocks called? The Water Table.

The "Roaring Forties" and "Furious Fifties" refer to exceptionally strong westerly winds found in which hemisphere? The Southern Hemisphere.

Which physical phenomenon is entirely responsible for the deflection of ocean currents and winds to the right in the Northern Hemisphere? The Coriolis Effect.

What is the standard instrument used to measure the intensity of solar radiation? A Pyranometer.

Which global climate classification system uses combinations of letters (like Aw, Cfa, BWh) to describe climates based on temperature and precipitation? The Köppen Climate Classification.

Which Indian state is entirely located in the Himalayas and shares its borders with Nepal, Tibet, and Bhutan? Sikkim.

The "Chilgoza" pine, known for its edible nuts, is a prominent tree found in which mountainous region of India? The Northwestern Himalayas (Himachal Pradesh and J&K).

Which type of seismic wave causes the most surface damage during an earthquake? Surface Waves (Love and Rayleigh waves).

In oceanography, what are the three main types of coral reefs? Fringing reefs, Barrier reefs, and Atolls.

Which Indian island group is entirely formed of coral deposits (atolls)? Lakshadweep.

What is the name of the deep, narrow ocean trenches that mark subduction zones where one tectonic plate is forced under another? Oceanic Trenches.

Which is the only Indian river to flow through three countries (China/Tibet, India, and Pakistan)? The Indus River (or the Sutlej/Brahmaputra depending on specific geographical definitions, but the Indus is the classic answer).

What is the highest point of the Aravalli Range, the oldest fold mountains in India? Guru Shikhar.

📜 Historical Background of the Constitution
Which British Act laid the first foundation of central administration in India by designating the Governor of Bengal as the "Governor-General of Bengal"? The Regulating Act of 1773.

Who was the first Governor-General of Bengal? Lord Warren Hastings.

Which Act established the first Supreme Court at Calcutta in 1774? The Regulating Act of 1773.

Which Act distinguished between the commercial and political functions of the East India Company by creating a Board of Control? Pitt's India Act of 1784.

Which Act made the Governor-General of Bengal the "Governor-General of India" and vested in him all civil and military powers? The Charter Act of 1833.

Who was the first Governor-General of India? Lord William Bentinck.

Which Act introduced an open competition system for the selection of civil servants? The Charter Act of 1853.

Which Act transferred the powers of government, territories, and revenues from the East India Company directly to the British Crown? The Government of India Act of 1858.

Who was the first Viceroy of India? Lord Canning.

Which Act introduced the system of "Separate Electorates" for Muslims, effectively legalizing communalism? The Indian Councils Act of 1909 (Morley-Minto Reforms).

Who is known as the "Father of Communal Electorate" in India? Lord Minto.

Which Act introduced "Dyarchy" (double rule) in the provinces, dividing subjects into "Reserved" and "Transferred"? The Government of India Act of 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms).

Which Act introduced bicameralism and direct elections in the country for the first time? The Government of India Act of 1919.

Which Act proposed the establishment of an "All-India Federation" consisting of provinces and princely states? The Government of India Act of 1935.

The Government of India Act of 1935 divided powers between the Centre and units into how many lists? Three (Federal List, Provincial List, and Concurrent List).

🏛️ Making of the Constitution
Who was the first person to put forward the idea of a Constituent Assembly for India in 1934? M.N. Roy.

Which British plan officially accepted the demand for a Constituent Assembly in 1940? The August Offer.

The Constituent Assembly was constituted in November 1946 under the scheme formulated by which mission? The Cabinet Mission Plan.

Who was elected as the temporary President of the Constituent Assembly in its first meeting on December 9, 1946? Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha.

Who was elected as the permanent President of the Constituent Assembly on December 11, 1946? Dr. Rajendra Prasad.

Who was appointed as the Constitutional Advisor to the Constituent Assembly? Sir B.N. Rau.

Who moved the historic "Objectives Resolution" in the Assembly on December 13, 1946? Jawaharlal Nehru.

The Constituent Assembly adopted the National Flag on which date? July 22, 1947.

The Constituent Assembly adopted the National Anthem and National Song on which date? January 24, 1950.

Who was the Chairman of the all-important Drafting Committee? Dr. B.R. Ambedkar.

How many members, including the Chairman, were there in the Drafting Committee? Seven.

Who was the Chairman of the Union Powers Committee and the Union Constitution Committee? Jawaharlal Nehru.

Who was the Chairman of the Provincial Constitution Committee? Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel.

Exactly how much time did it take to frame the Constitution of India? 2 years, 11 months, and 18 days.

On which exact date was the Constitution of India adopted and enacted by the Constituent Assembly? November 26, 1949.

🌐 Salient Features & Sources of the Constitution
The structural part of the Indian Constitution is largely derived from which historical Act? The Government of India Act of 1935.

The concept of Fundamental Rights was borrowed from the constitution of which country? United States of America (USA).

The Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP) were borrowed from which country? Ireland.

The Parliamentary system of government, Rule of Law, and Single Citizenship were borrowed from where? The United Kingdom (Britain).

The concept of the Concurrent List and the joint sitting of the two houses of Parliament were borrowed from which country? Australia.

The idea of a Federation with a strong Centre was borrowed from which country? Canada.

The Fundamental Duties and the ideal of justice (social, economic, and political) in the Preamble were borrowed from where? USSR (Soviet Union).

The procedure for the amendment of the Constitution was borrowed from which country? South Africa.

The ideals of Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity in the Preamble were taken from which major historical event? The French Revolution.

How many Schedules were there in the original Constitution of 1949? Eight.

How many Schedules does the Indian Constitution currently have? Twelve.

Which Schedule of the Constitution deals with the allocation of seats in the Rajya Sabha? The Fourth Schedule.

Which Schedule contains the provisions relating to the administration of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes? The Fifth Schedule.

Which Schedule deals specifically with the tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram? The Sixth Schedule.

Which Schedule divides legislative powers between the Union and the States (Union List, State List, Concurrent List)? The Seventh Schedule.

📖 The Preamble & The Union and its Territory
The Preamble is a modified version of which historic document? The Objectives Resolution.

How many times has the Preamble been amended so far? Only once.

Which Constitutional Amendment Act amended the Preamble in 1976? The 42nd Amendment Act.

Which three new words were added to the Preamble by the 42nd Amendment Act? Socialist, Secular, and Integrity.

In which landmark case did the Supreme Court explicitly rule that the Preamble is a part of the Constitution? Kesavananda Bharati Case (1973).

Article 1 of the Constitution describes India, that is Bharat, as what? A Union of States.

Which Article grants the Parliament the power to admit new states into the Union or establish new states? Article 2.

Which Article authorizes the Parliament to form a new state by separation of territory, or alter the boundaries/names of existing states? Article 3.

Who appointed the Linguistic Provinces Commission (Dhar Commission) in 1948 to examine the feasibility of organizing states on a linguistic basis? The Constituent Assembly (Dr. Rajendra Prasad).

Which was the first state in independent India to be created on a linguistic basis? Andhra Pradesh (1953).

Who was the Chairman of the States Reorganisation Commission formed in 1953? Fazl Ali.

The States Reorganisation Act of 1956 created how many states and union territories? 14 States and 6 Union Territories.

Which Constitutional Amendment Act incorporated Sikkim as a full-fledged State of the Indian Union? The 36th Amendment Act (1975).

Which is the latest state to be created in India (in 2014)? Telangana.

Which Act bifurcated the State of Jammu and Kashmir into two Union Territories in 2019? The Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019.

🛂 Citizenship
Which Part of the Indian Constitution deals with Citizenship? Part II (Articles 5 to 11).

Does the Indian Constitution provide for dual citizenship (state and national)? No, it provides for Single Citizenship.

Which Article explicitly grants the Parliament the power to regulate the right of citizenship by law? Article 11.

Which comprehensive Act was passed by the Parliament to regulate the acquisition and loss of citizenship after the commencement of the Constitution? The Citizenship Act, 1955.

Under the Citizenship Act of 1955, how many ways are there to acquire Indian citizenship? Five (Birth, Descent, Registration, Naturalisation, and Incorporation of territory).

What are the three ways by which an Indian citizen can lose their citizenship? Renunciation, Termination, and Deprivation.

Who is considered the first citizen of India? The President of India.

NRI stands for Non-Resident Indian. What does OCI stand for? Overseas Citizen of India.

Do OCI cardholders have the right to vote in Indian elections? No.

Can an OCI cardholder buy agricultural land or plantation property in India? No.

🛡️ Fundamental Rights (The Core)
Which Part of the Constitution is often described as the "Magna Carta of India"? Part III (Fundamental Rights).

Fundamental Rights are enshrined in which Articles of the Constitution? Articles 12 to 35.

Are Fundamental Rights absolute? No, they are subject to reasonable restrictions.

Are Fundamental Rights justifiable (enforceable by courts)? Yes.

Which Article defines the term "State" for the purposes of Part III? Article 12.

Which Article declares that all laws that are inconsistent with or in derogation of any of the fundamental rights shall be void? Article 13.

Article 14 guarantees which two fundamental concepts regarding equality? Equality before the law and equal protection of the laws.

Which Article prohibits discrimination on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth? Article 15.

Which Article guarantees equality of opportunity in matters of public employment? Article 16.

Which Article abolishes "Untouchability" and forbids its practice in any form? Article 17.

Which Article abolishes titles (except military and academic distinctions)? Article 18.

Article 19 originally guaranteed seven rights. How many does it guarantee now? Six (The Right to Property was removed).

Which Amendment Act deleted the Right to Property from the list of Fundamental Rights? The 44th Amendment Act (1978).

What is the current constitutional status of the Right to Property? It is a legal right under Article 300-A in Part XII of the Constitution.

Which Article provides protection in respect of conviction for offences (including protection against double jeopardy and self-incrimination)? Article 20.

Which Article guarantees the protection of life and personal liberty? Article 21.

In which landmark case did the Supreme Court interpret the "Right to Life" broadly to include the right to live with human dignity? Maneka Gandhi Case (1978).

Which Constitutional Amendment added Article 21A, making elementary education a fundamental right? The 86th Amendment Act (2002).

Article 21A guarantees free and compulsory education to all children of what age group? 6 to 14 years.

Which Article grants protection against arbitrary arrest and detention? Article 22.

Which Article strictly prohibits traffic in human beings and forced labor (begar)? Article 23.

Which Article prohibits the employment of children below the age of 14 years in factories, mines, or other hazardous activities? Article 24.

Which Articles guarantee the Right to Freedom of Religion? Articles 25 to 28.

Which Article guarantees the right of minorities to establish and administer educational institutions? Article 30.

Which Article is called the "Heart and Soul of the Constitution" by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar? Article 32 (Right to Constitutional Remedies).

Under Article 32, who has the power to issue writs for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights? The Supreme Court of India.

Under which Article do High Courts have the concurrent power to issue writs? Article 226.

Which writ literally means "we command" and is issued by a court to a public official asking them to perform their official duties? Mandamus.

Which writ is issued by a higher court to a lower court to prevent it from exceeding its jurisdiction? Prohibition.

Which writ literally means "by what authority or warrant" and is used to inquire into the legality of a claim of a person to a public office? Quo-Warranto.

You just crushed the first 100 Polity questions! This covers the foundational structure and the most tested articles of the Constitution.

Here is Part 2 (Questions 101–200) of your Polity master-list. This section dives deep into the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP), Fundamental Duties, the Union Executive (President & PM), the Parliament, and the Supreme Court.

These are the core administrative mechanisms of the country, so pay close attention to the specific Articles and Amendments!

🧭 Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP) & Fundamental Duties
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP)? Part IV (Articles 36 to 51).

Are the Directive Principles enforceable by the courts? No, they are non-justiciable.

Which Article explicitly directs the State to organize village panchayats and endow them with necessary powers? Article 40.

Which Article directs the State to secure a Uniform Civil Code (UCC) for the citizens throughout the territory of India? Article 44.

Which Article deals with the separation of the judiciary from the executive in the public services of the State? Article 50.

Which Article promotes international peace and security? Article 51.

Which Amendment Act added the provision for "equal justice and free legal aid" (Article 39A)? The 42nd Amendment Act (1976).

Which Amendment Act added the directive to "minimize inequalities in income, status, facilities, and opportunities" (Article 38)? The 44th Amendment Act (1978).

Which Part of the Constitution contains the Fundamental Duties? Part IVA (Article 51A).

Which committee officially recommended the incorporation of Fundamental Duties into the Constitution? The Swaran Singh Committee.

Which Amendment Act originally added the Fundamental Duties to the Constitution in 1976? The 42nd Amendment Act.

How many Fundamental Duties are there currently? 11.

Which amendment added the 11th Fundamental Duty (regarding providing opportunities for education to children between 6 and 14 years)? The 86th Amendment Act (2002).

👑 The Union Executive: President & Vice-President
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the Union Executive? Part V (Articles 52 to 78).

Who is the constitutional head of the Indian State and the Supreme Commander of the Armed Forces? The President of India.

What is the minimum age required to be eligible for election as the President? 35 years.

Who makes up the Electoral College that elects the President? Elected members of both Houses of Parliament, and elected members of the legislative assemblies of the states (including Delhi and Puducherry). Note: Nominated members do not participate.

Which voting system is used for the election of the President? Proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote.

Which Article details the specific procedure for the impeachment of the President? Article 61.

On what sole ground can the President be impeached? "Violation of the Constitution."

Who formally administers the oath of office to the President? The Chief Justice of India.

Under which Article does the President have the absolute power to grant pardons, reprieves, or remissions of punishment? Article 72.

Under which Article can the President promulgate Ordinances when Parliament is not in session? Article 123.

Who is the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha? The Vice-President of India.

Who acts as the President when a vacancy occurs due to resignation, removal, death, or otherwise? The Vice-President.

What is the minimum age required to become the Vice-President? 35 years.

Who makes up the Electoral College that elects the Vice-President? All members (both elected and nominated) of both Houses of Parliament.

🏛️ Prime Minister, Council of Ministers & Attorney General
While the President is the nominal executive authority (de jure), who is the real executive authority (de facto)? The Prime Minister.

Which Article states that there shall be a Council of Ministers with the Prime Minister at the head to aid and advise the President? Article 74.

Who formally appoints the Prime Minister? The President.

The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to which house of Parliament? The Lok Sabha.

Under which Article is the "collective responsibility" of the Council of Ministers specifically defined? Article 75.

What is the maximum allowed size of the Council of Ministers (including the PM) as a percentage of the total strength of the Lok Sabha? 15%.

Which Constitutional Amendment Act capped the size of the Council of Ministers at 15%? The 91st Amendment Act (2003).

Who is the highest law officer in the country? The Attorney General of India.

Which Article deals with the appointment and duties of the Attorney General? Article 76.

Does the Attorney General have the right to speak and take part in the proceedings of both Houses of Parliament? Yes, but they do not have the right to vote.

🗳️ The Parliament of India
Under Article 79, the Parliament of India consists of what three components? The President, the Council of States (Rajya Sabha), and the House of the People (Lok Sabha).

What is the maximum sanctioned constitutional strength of the Rajya Sabha? 250 members.

How many members are directly nominated to the Rajya Sabha by the President? 12 members.

On what grounds are these 12 members nominated? For having special knowledge or practical experience in Art, Literature, Science, and Social Service.

What is the maximum sanctioned constitutional strength of the Lok Sabha today? 550 members (previously 552 before the Anglo-Indian seats were removed).

Which amendment officially abolished the nomination of two Anglo-Indians to the Lok Sabha? The 104th Amendment Act (2019).

What is the normal term of the Lok Sabha before it is dissolved? 5 years.

What is the term of an individual member of the Rajya Sabha? 6 years (with one-third of members retiring every second year).

Who presides over a joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament? The Speaker of the Lok Sabha.

Which Article provides the President the power to summon a joint sitting of both Houses to resolve a legislative deadlock? Article 108.

Which Article explicitly defines a "Money Bill"? Article 110.

Who holds the final authority to decide whether a bill is a Money Bill or not? The Speaker of the Lok Sabha.

Can a Money Bill be introduced in the Rajya Sabha? No, it can only be introduced in the Lok Sabha (on the recommendation of the President).

What is the maximum number of days the Rajya Sabha can delay a Money Bill before it is deemed passed? 14 days.

In parliamentary proceedings, what is the first hour of a sitting normally devoted to? Question Hour.

What is the uniquely Indian parliamentary innovation that follows the Question Hour, where members can raise urgent matters without prior notice? Zero Hour.

What is the minimum number of members required to be present to hold a meeting of the House (Quorum)? One-tenth (1/10th) of the total number of members.

Which major fund contains all revenues received by the Government of India, and from which no money can be withdrawn without parliamentary approval? The Consolidated Fund of India (Article 266).

Which fund is placed at the disposal of the President to meet unforeseen, urgent expenditures pending parliamentary authorization? The Contingency Fund of India (Article 267).

Under which Article is the "Annual Financial Statement" (Union Budget) presented to Parliament? Article 112.

Which parliamentary motion is moved by the opposition to reduce the amount of a demand for a grant in the budget? Cut Motion.

Which motion can be introduced only in the Lok Sabha to express a lack of confidence in the Council of Ministers? No-Confidence Motion.

The support of how many members is required to admit a No-Confidence Motion in the Lok Sabha? 50 members.

Which crucial parliamentary committee examines the annual audit reports submitted by the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG)? The Public Accounts Committee (PAC).

How many members are there in the Public Accounts Committee? 22 members (15 from Lok Sabha and 7 from Rajya Sabha).

Which is the largest parliamentary committee, consisting of 30 members all exclusively from the Lok Sabha? The Estimates Committee.

⚖️ The Supreme Court & Judiciary
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the Union Judiciary (the Supreme Court)? Part V.

In which year was the Supreme Court of India officially inaugurated? January 28, 1950.

Which Article deals with the establishment and constitution of the Supreme Court? Article 124.

Who formally appoints the Judges of the Supreme Court? The President.

What is the mandatory retirement age for a judge of the Supreme Court? 65 years.

What is the informal system called where a group of senior judges (led by the CJI) recommends the appointments and transfers of judges? The Collegium System.

Which Constitutional Amendment sought to replace the Collegium System with the National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) but was struck down as unconstitutional by the Supreme Court? The 99th Amendment Act (2014).

Which jurisdiction gives the Supreme Court the exclusive power to hear disputes between the Centre and States, or between two or more States? Original Jurisdiction (Article 131).

Under which Article can the President seek the advisory opinion of the Supreme Court on questions of law or public importance? Article 143 (Advisory Jurisdiction).

What is the power of the Supreme Court to examine the constitutionality of legislative enactments and executive orders called? Judicial Review.

Which Article gives the Supreme Court the power to review its own previous judgments or orders? Article 137.

Which two legendary Justices are widely credited with introducing the concept of Public Interest Litigation (PIL) in India? Justice P.N. Bhagwati and Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer.

🏛️ State Executive & Legislature
Who is the nominal executive head of a State in India? The Governor.

Which Article states that there shall be a Governor for each state? Article 153.

Which Amendment Act facilitated the appointment of the same person as a Governor for two or more states? The 7th Amendment Act (1956).

Who appoints the Governor of a state? The President (by warrant under his hand and seal).

What is the minimum age required to be appointed as a Governor? 35 years.

Does the Constitution explicitly lay down the grounds for the removal of the Governor? No. (The Governor holds office during the pleasure of the President).

Which Article gives the Governor the power to grant pardons or suspend/remit sentences? Article 161.

Can the Governor pardon a death sentence? No. (Even if a state law prescribes a death sentence, the power to pardon it lies exclusively with the President).

Under which Article can the Governor promulgate ordinances when the state legislature is not in session? Article 213.

Who is the real executive head (de facto) of the state government? The Chief Minister.

Which Article provides that there shall be a Council of Ministers with the Chief Minister at the head to aid and advise the Governor? Article 163.

Who is the highest law officer in a State? The Advocate General.

Which Article deals with the appointment and duties of the Advocate General of the State? Article 165.

Currently, how many states in India have a bicameral legislature (both an Assembly and a Council)? Six (Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Maharashtra, and Karnataka).

Which Article deals with the creation or abolition of Legislative Councils in states? Article 169.

What is the Legislative Assembly of a state called in Hindi? Vidhan Sabha.

What is the Legislative Council of a state called in Hindi? Vidhan Parishad.

What is the maximum permitted constitutional strength of a State Legislative Assembly? 500 members.

What is the minimum permitted constitutional strength of a State Legislative Assembly (excluding special exceptions like Goa or Sikkim)? 60 members.

The total size of the Legislative Council cannot exceed what fraction of the total strength of the Legislative Assembly of that state? One-third (1/3rd).

Who administers the oath of office and secrecy to the Chief Minister? The Governor.

If an ordinary bill originates in the Legislative Assembly, what is the maximum duration the Legislative Council can delay it in the first instance? 3 months.

Under which Article can the Governor reserve a bill passed by the state legislature for the consideration of the President? Article 200.

What is the maximum time a state Legislative Council can delay a Money Bill? 14 days.

Does a state legislature have the power to initiate a constitutional amendment? No. (Only Parliament can initiate constitutional amendments).

You are blazing through this! You now have a rock-solid grip on the Union and State machinery.

Here is Part 3 (Questions 201–300) of your Polity master-list. This section is incredibly high-yield for both UPSC and UKPSC Prelims. We are going to cover the High Courts, Panchayati Raj (Local Government), Centre-State Relations, Emergency Provisions, and the major Constitutional Bodies.

Let's keep building that fortress!

⚖️ High Courts & Subordinate Courts
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the High Courts in the states? Part VI.

Which is the oldest High Court in India, established in 1862? The Calcutta High Court.

Which Article states that there shall be a High Court for each state? Article 214.

Which Article allows Parliament to establish a common High Court for two or more states? Article 231.

Who officially appoints the Chief Justice and judges of a High Court? The President of India.

Who administers the oath of office to a judge of a High Court? The Governor of the State.

What is the mandatory retirement age for a judge of a High Court? 62 years.

Can a High Court judge be removed by the Governor? No. (They can only be removed by the President on the recommendation of Parliament).

Which Union Territory has its own independent High Court? Delhi (and Jammu & Kashmir).

Under which Article do High Courts have the power to issue writs? Article 226.

Is the writ jurisdiction of the High Court wider than that of the Supreme Court? Yes. (The Supreme Court can issue writs only for Fundamental Rights, while High Courts can issue them for Fundamental Rights and other legal rights).

Who has the power to transfer a judge from one High Court to another? The President of India (after consulting the Chief Justice of India).

Who appoints the District Judges in a state? The Governor (in consultation with the High Court).

What is the highest criminal court at the district level called? The Sessions Court.

In which year was the Gram Nyayalayas Act passed to provide access to justice at the grassroots level? 2008.

🏘️ Panchayati Raj & Municipalities (Local Government)
Which Constitutional Amendment Act gave constitutional status to the Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs)? The 73rd Amendment Act (1992).

Which Part was added to the Constitution by the 73rd Amendment Act? Part IX ("The Panchayats").

Which Schedule was added to the Constitution by the 73rd Amendment Act? The 11th Schedule.

How many functional items are listed in the 11th Schedule for the Panchayats? 29 items.

Which state was the first to establish Panchayati Raj in independent India (in Nagaur district, 1959)? Rajasthan.

Which committee in 1957 recommended the establishment of a democratic decentralized three-tier Panchayati Raj system? The Balwant Rai Mehta Committee.

Which committee in 1977 recommended replacing the three-tier system with a two-tier system? The Ashok Mehta Committee.

What is the minimum age required to contest Panchayat elections? 21 years.

What fraction of seats is constitutionally reserved for women in Panchayats at all levels? Not less than one-third (33%).

Who is responsible for conducting the elections to the Panchayats and Municipalities? The State Election Commission.

Which Article establishes the State Election Commission? Article 243K.

Who constitutes the State Finance Commission every five years to review the financial position of the Panchayats? The Governor.

Which Constitutional Amendment Act gave constitutional status to Municipalities (Urban Local Bodies)? The 74th Amendment Act (1992).

Which Part and Schedule were added by the 74th Amendment Act? Part IX-A and the 12th Schedule.

How many functional items are listed in the 12th Schedule for Municipalities? 18 items.

Which city established the first Municipal Corporation in India during British rule (1687)? Madras (Chennai).

🤝 Centre-State Relations
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the legislative and administrative relations between the Centre and the States? Part XI.

Which Schedule details the division of legislative powers between the Union and the States? The 7th Schedule.

Who holds the power to legislate on "Residuary Subjects" (matters not enumerated in any of the three lists)? The Parliament (Article 248).

Under Article 249, Parliament can legislate on a subject in the State List if which house passes a resolution supported by a two-thirds majority? The Rajya Sabha.

Can the Parliament legislate on subjects in the State List during a National Emergency? Yes (under Article 250).

Which Article provides for the adjudication of inter-state water disputes? Article 262.

Which Article grants the President the power to establish an Inter-State Council to promote coordination between states? Article 263.

Who acts as the Chairman of the Inter-State Council? The Prime Minister.

Are the "Zonal Councils" constitutional bodies or statutory bodies? Statutory bodies (established by the States Reorganisation Act of 1956).

Who acts as the common Chairman of all Zonal Councils? The Union Home Minister.

Which major commission was appointed by the Central Government in 1983 to review Centre-State relations? The Sarkaria Commission.

Which commission was appointed in 2007 to look into the new issues of Centre-State relations? The Punchhi Commission.

🚨 Emergency Provisions
Which Part of the Constitution deals with Emergency Provisions? Part XVIII.

Which Article details the proclamation of a National Emergency? Article 352.

What are the three constitutional grounds for declaring a National Emergency? War, external aggression, or armed rebellion.

Which Amendment Act substituted the phrase "internal disturbance" with "armed rebellion"? The 44th Amendment Act (1978).

How many times has a National Emergency been declared in India? Three times (1962, 1971, and 1975).

Which Article provides for the imposition of President's Rule (State Emergency) due to the failure of constitutional machinery in a state? Article 356.

Which state was the first to see the imposition of President's Rule in 1951? Punjab.

What is the maximum duration that President's Rule can remain in force in a state (with parliamentary approval every six months)? 3 years.

Which Article empowers the President to declare a Financial Emergency? Article 360.

Has a Financial Emergency ever been declared in India? No.

Under Article 358, which Fundamental Rights are automatically suspended when a National Emergency is declared on grounds of war or external aggression? The six fundamental rights under Article 19.

Which two Articles containing Fundamental Rights can never be suspended during any emergency? Articles 20 and 21.

🏛️ Constitutional Bodies
Which Article establishes the Election Commission of India? Article 324.

What is the current composition of the Election Commission? One Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) and two Election Commissioners.

Who appoints the Chief Election Commissioner and other Election Commissioners? The President.

What is the tenure of the Chief Election Commissioner? 6 years or up to the age of 65 years, whichever is earlier.

The procedure for removing the Chief Election Commissioner is identical to the removal of whom? A judge of the Supreme Court.

Which Article establishes the Union Public Service Commission (UPSC)? Article 315.

Who appoints the Chairman and members of the UPSC? The President.

What is the tenure of the Chairman of the UPSC? 6 years or up to the age of 65 years.

Who can remove the Chairman of the UPSC from office? The President (on grounds of proved misbehavior, after an inquiry by the Supreme Court).

Who appoints the Chairman and members of a State Public Service Commission (SPSC)? The Governor.

Who holds the power to remove the Chairman of an SPSC? The President (not the Governor).

Which Article deals with the establishment of the Finance Commission? Article 280.

Who constitutes the Finance Commission, and how often? The President, every fifth year (or earlier if necessary).

What is the primary function of the Finance Commission? To recommend the distribution of the net proceeds of taxes between the Centre and the States.

Who was the Chairman of the First Finance Commission of India? K.C. Neogy.

Who has been appointed as the Chairman of the 16th Finance Commission (constituted recently for the 2026-31 period)? Dr. Arvind Panagariya.

Which Article establishes the National Commission for Scheduled Castes (NCSC)? Article 338.

Which Article establishes the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes (NCST)? Article 338A.

Which Article establishes the National Commission for Backward Classes (NCBC)? Article 338B.

Which Constitutional Amendment Act granted constitutional status to the NCBC? The 102nd Amendment Act (2018).

Which Article establishes the office of the Special Officer for Linguistic Minorities? Article 350B.

Which Article establishes the office of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG)? Article 148.

Who appoints the CAG? The President.

What is the tenure of the CAG? 6 years or up to the age of 65 years.

To whom does the CAG submit the annual audit reports relating to the accounts of the Union? The President.

The CAG acts as a guide, friend, and philosopher to which important parliamentary committee? The Public Accounts Committee (PAC).

Does the CAG have control over the issue of money from the Consolidated Fund of India? No. (The CAG in India only audits the accounts after the expenditure is committed, unlike the CAG in Britain).

Who is the chief legal advisor to the Government of India? The Attorney General (Article 76).

What are the necessary qualifications to be appointed as the Attorney General of India? Must be qualified to be appointed as a judge of the Supreme Court.

Is the Attorney General a full-time government servant? No. (They are not debarred from private legal practice).

Who is the corresponding chief legal advisor to the State Government? The Advocate General of the State (Article 165).

🌐 Official Languages & Amendment of the Constitution
Which Part of the Constitution deals with the Official Language? Part XVII.

Which Article designates Hindi written in the Devanagari script as the official language of the Union? Article 343.

Which Schedule of the Constitution lists the recognized official languages of India? The 8th Schedule.

How many languages are currently recognized in the 8th Schedule? 22 languages.

Which Amendment Act added Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, and Santhali to the 8th Schedule? The 92nd Amendment Act (2003).

Which Article directs the Union government to promote the spread and development of the Hindi language? Article 351.

Which classical language was the first to be officially recognized by the Government of India in 2004? Tamil.

Which Part of the Constitution deals with its Amendment? Part XX.

Which specific Article details the procedure for the Amendment of the Constitution? Article 368.

Can a constitutional amendment bill be introduced in a State Legislature? No. (It can only be introduced in either House of Parliament).

Does a constitutional amendment bill require the prior permission of the President before introduction? No.

Can the President withhold assent to a constitutional amendment bill? No. (The 24th Amendment Act of 1971 made it obligatory for the President to give his assent).

If a constitutional amendment seeks to change the federal provisions of the Constitution, what additional approval is required? Ratification by the legislatures of at least half of the states.

Can Parliament amend the "Basic Structure" of the Constitution? No. (As ruled by the Supreme Court in the Kesavananda Bharati case).
