🌾 Agricultural Economics & Food Security
Which institution is responsible for recommending the Minimum Support Price (MSP) for 22 mandated crops in India? Commission for Agricultural Costs and Prices (CACP).

Who takes the final decision on approving the Minimum Support Price (MSP)? The Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA), chaired by the Prime Minister.

For sugarcane, the government announces a different pricing metric instead of MSP. What is it called? Fair and Remunerative Price (FRP).

What is the "A2 + FL" cost formula used in calculating MSP? It includes all out-of-pocket expenses incurred by farmers (A2) plus the imputed value of unpaid family labor (FL).

The Swaminathan Committee famously recommended that the MSP should be at least how much percentage more than the weighted average cost of production (C2)? 50% more.

Which central agency is primarily responsible for the procurement, storage, and distribution of food grains in India? Food Corporation of India (FCI).

What is the "Issue Price" in the context of the Public Distribution System (PDS)? The heavily subsidized price at which FCI sells food grains to the State Governments for distribution through ration shops.

What are "Buffer Stocks"? The reserve of food grains (like wheat and rice) maintained by the government through FCI to ensure food security during emergencies or crop failures.

What is the e-NAM portal? National Agriculture Market, a pan-India electronic trading portal that networks existing APMC mandis to create a unified national market for agricultural commodities.

What does APMC stand for? Agricultural Produce Market Committee (statutory market boards established by state governments).

In the context of WTO agricultural subsidies, what is the "Amber Box"? Subsidies that distort trade and production (e.g., direct price supports like MSP, fertilizer subsidies). These are subject to reduction commitments.

What is the "Green Box" in WTO terminology? Subsidies that do not distort trade, or at most cause minimal distortion (e.g., environmental protection programs, agricultural research). These are allowed without limits.

What is the "Blue Box" in WTO terminology? Essentially the "Amber Box with conditions"—subsidies that are tied to programs that limit production.

What is "Contract Farming"? Agricultural production carried out according to an agreement between a buyer and farmers, establishing conditions for the production and marketing of a farm product.

What is the primary aim of the Paramparagat Krishi Vikas Yojana (PKVY)? To promote organic farming in India.

Which Indian state was the first to be declared a "100% Organic Farming State"? Sikkim.

What is "Zero Budget Natural Farming" (ZBNF)? A method of chemical-free agriculture drawing from traditional Indian practices, avoiding the use of synthetic fertilizers and pesticides.

The "Operation Flood" program was launched to revolutionize which sector in India? The Dairy Sector (Milk production).

Who is known as the "Milkman of India" and the architect of Operation Flood? Dr. Verghese Kurien.

What is the "Evergreen Revolution," a term coined by M.S. Swaminathan? Enhancing agricultural productivity in perpetuity without causing ecological harm.

What is the "Kisan Credit Card" (KCC) scheme? A scheme that provides farmers with timely access to short-term credit from the banking system for agricultural expenses.

What does the acronym NABARD stand for? National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development.

In which year was NABARD established? 1982 (on the recommendation of the B. Sivaraman Committee).

NABARD manages the RIDF, which provides loans to State Governments for rural infrastructure. What does RIDF stand for? Rural Infrastructure Development Fund.

What is "Precision Agriculture"? A farming management concept utilizing technology (like GPS, IoT, and drones) to observe, measure, and respond to inter and intra-field variability in crops to optimize returns.

🏦 Financial Institutions, Indices & Banking Operations
Which institution serves as the apex body for regulating the housing finance sector in India? National Housing Bank (NHB).

The Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) was set up as a wholly-owned subsidiary of which institution? IDBI Bank.

Where is the headquarters of SIDBI located? Lucknow.

What is the Export-Import Bank of India (EXIM Bank) primarily responsible for? Providing financial assistance to exporters and importers to promote India's foreign trade.

What is "Core Banking Solution" (CBS)? A networking of bank branches that allows customers to manage their accounts and access banking services from any branch of that bank on the CBS network.

What does IFSC stand for in the Indian banking system? Indian Financial System Code (an 11-digit alphanumeric code used to identify bank branches for online fund transfers like NEFT and RTGS).

What is MICR code used for? Magnetic Ink Character Recognition (a 9-digit code printed on cheques to facilitate faster processing and clearance).

In electronic fund transfers, what is the key difference between NEFT and RTGS? RTGS (Real-Time Gross Settlement) happens in real-time and is typically for high-value transactions, while NEFT (National Electronic Funds Transfer) processes transactions in half-hourly batches.

What does NPCI stand for, the umbrella organization for operating retail payments and settlement systems in India? National Payments Corporation of India.

Which revolutionary instant real-time payment system was developed by NPCI in 2016? Unified Payments Interface (UPI).

What is a "Payment Bank"? A differentiated bank that can accept restricted deposits (up to Rs. 2 lakh) and offer remittance services, but cannot issue loans or credit cards.

What is a "Small Finance Bank"? A niche bank that provides basic banking activities like accepting deposits and lending to unserved and underserved sections (micro-industries, small farmers).

What is "Priority Sector Lending" (PSL)? An RBI mandate requiring commercial banks to dedicate a specific portion (usually 40%) of their total adjusted net bank credit to specific sectors like agriculture, MSMEs, education, and housing.

What does "BASEL Norms" refer to in the banking sector? International banking regulations issued by the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision (BCBS) to ensure banks maintain enough capital to meet their obligations and absorb unexpected losses.

What is the "Capital Adequacy Ratio" (CAR)? The ratio of a bank's capital to its risk-weighted assets, used to protect depositors and promote the stability and efficiency of financial systems.

What is a "Deemat Account" (Dematerialized Account)? An account used to hold shares and securities in an electronic format.

What are "Masala Bonds"? Rupee-denominated bonds issued outside India by Indian entities to raise funds in overseas markets.

What is a "Participatory Note" (P-Note)? A financial instrument used by overseas investors to invest in the Indian stock market without directly registering with SEBI.

Which index calculates the performance of the top 50 large-cap companies listed on the National Stock Exchange (NSE)? Nifty 50.

What is the Consumer Confidence Index (CCI), and who publishes it in India? A survey that measures how optimistic consumers feel about the economy and their personal financial situation; published by the RBI.

🌍 International Economic Reports & Organizations
Which organization publishes the "World Economic Outlook" report? International Monetary Fund (IMF).

Which organization publishes the "Global Financial Stability Report"? International Monetary Fund (IMF).

Which organization publishes the "World Development Report"? The World Bank.

Which organization traditionally published the "Ease of Doing Business" report (discontinued in 2021)? The World Bank.

The "Human Development Index" (HDI) is published by which organization? United Nations Development Programme (UNDP).

What are the three core dimensions used to calculate the Human Development Index (HDI)? Life expectancy (health), Education (years of schooling), and Gross National Income per capita (standard of living).

Which organization publishes the "Global Competitiveness Report"? World Economic Forum (WEF).

Which organization publishes the "Global Gender Gap Report"? World Economic Forum (WEF).

Which UN body publishes the "World Investment Report," focusing on Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) trends? UNCTAD (United Nations Conference on Trade and Development).

Where is the headquarters of the World Economic Forum (WEF)? Cologny (Geneva), Switzerland (its famous annual meeting is held in Davos).

What is the FATF (Financial Action Task Force)? An intergovernmental organization founded to develop policies to combat money laundering and the financing of terrorism.

If a country is placed on the FATF "Grey List," what does it signify? The country is under increased monitoring due to strategic deficiencies in its regimes to counter money laundering and terrorist financing.

Which organization serves as the central bank for the world's central banks and fosters international monetary and financial cooperation? Bank for International Settlements (BIS), headquartered in Basel, Switzerland.

Which agreement replaced the North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA)? USMCA (United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement).

What is OPEC? Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries, a cartel that aims to manage the supply of oil to set the price on the world market.

🏔️ Uttarakhand Economy: Infrastructure, GI Tags & New Initiatives
The "Manaskhand Mandir Mala Mission" is a major state economic initiative aimed at developing religious tourism infrastructure in which specific region? The Kumaon Division.

Similarly, the development of the Char Dham and surrounding ancient temples in Garhwal is promoted under which regional circuit? The Kedarkhand Circuit.

Under the National Ropeways Development Programme (Parvatmala), a major ropeway is being constructed to connect Govindghat to which revered high-altitude pilgrimage site? Hemkund Sahib.

Another major ropeway project under Parvatmala will connect Gaurikund directly to which shrine, drastically reducing the trekking time? Kedarnath.

Under the Central Government's "Vibrant Villages Programme," the border village of Mana (Chamoli) has been officially re-designated from the "Last Village of India" to what? The "First Village of India" (to boost border tourism and economic activity).

To curb rural out-migration, the Uttarakhand government launched the "Lakhpati Didi" scheme. It aims to empower women associated with which grassroots organizations? Self-Help Groups (SHGs), targeting an annual income of over Rs. 1 lakh per woman.

Uttarakhand recently secured Geographical Indication (GI) tags for 18 local products in a single day. "Beri Nag Tea," which received a GI tag, is native to which district? Pithoragarh.

Which highly nutritious local red rice from Uttarkashi district received a GI tag? Purola Lal Dhan.

"Buransh" (Rhododendron) squash/juice is a major micro-economic forest product of the state. What color is the classic Buransh flower? Crimson red.

Which GI-tagged traditional sweet of Uttarakhand is made using roasted khoya coated with white sugar balls? Almora Bal Mithai.

Which traditional Kumaoni garment, essentially a yellow dupatta with red polka dots worn during auspicious occasions, received a GI tag? Kumaoni Pichhaura (Rangwali Pichhaura).

What is the primary economic purpose of the "Mukhyamantri Matsya Sampada Yojana" in Uttarakhand? To boost fish farming, aquaculture, and provide financial assistance to local fishermen in the state.

To harness its massive hydropower potential while maintaining ecological balance, Uttarakhand operates several "Run-of-the-River" (RoR) projects. What does RoR mean? Hydroelectric generation whereby little or no water storage is provided, utilizing the natural flow and elevation drop of a river.

The upcoming "Rishikesh-Karnaprayag Railway Line" will feature India's longest railway tunnel (Tunnel 8). Between which two stations is this massive tunnel located? Devprayag and Janasu.

Which specific airport in Uttarakhand is being aggressively expanded to serve as an international airport to boost global tourism and cargo exports? Jolly Grant Airport (Dehradun).

Pantnagar Airport is located in which highly industrialized district of Uttarakhand? Udham Singh Nagar.

The Uttarakhand government heavily promotes "Homestays" to reverse migration. What financial incentive is typically provided under the Deendayal Upadhyay Homestay Scheme? Capital subsidy up to 33% (or up to Rs 10 Lakhs) for building or upgrading homestays in hilly areas, plus interest subvention.

What is the overarching focus of the state's "Aroma Park" established in Kashipur? To boost the cultivation and processing of aromatic plants (like lemongrass, mint, and damask rose) for essential oils and perfumes.

Which board was constituted to specifically protect and promote the rights and economic welfare of workers in the unorganized sector (like construction workers) in Uttarakhand? Uttarakhand Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Board.

What is the "Chief Minister's Self-Employment Scheme" (Mukhyamantri Swarojgar Yojana - MSY)? A scheme to provide subsidized credit to returning migrants and local youth to set up manufacturing and service enterprises.

Uttarakhand heavily relies on the "Finance Commission Grants." The state falls under which category that guarantees extra central assistance to offset its geographical disadvantages? Special Category State.

Which local pulse, commonly grown in the terraced fields of Garhwal and Kumaon, is famous for its high protein content and is known locally as "Gahat" or "Kulthi"? Horse Gram.

The "Himalayan State Regional Council" was constituted under the aegis of which central body to ensure sustainable economic development of the Indian Himalayan Region? NITI Aayog.

Which state agency handles the marketing and distribution of local handicrafts, handlooms, and organic products under the brand name "Himadri"? Uttarakhand Handloom and Handicraft Development Council (UHHDC).

What is "Eco-Tourism"? Responsible travel to natural areas that conserves the environment, sustains the well-being of the local people, and involves interpretation and education. (A massive economic driver for Uttarakhand's protected areas like Corbett and Nanda Devi).

Which district in Uttarakhand is globally recognized for its export-quality "Litchi" production? Nainital (specifically the Ramnagar region).

The Uttarakhand government established a dedicated "Snow Leopard Conservation Centre" in which district, simultaneously creating a unique wildlife tourism niche? Uttarkashi (in Lanka, near Bhaironghati).

What is the primary objective of the "Uttarakhand Startup Policy"? To incubate innovation, provide seed funding, and create an entrepreneurship ecosystem in sectors like IT, healthcare, and agri-tech.

To promote border economy, the state organizes trade fairs with neighboring countries. Which historical pass in Pithoragarh was a major route for the Indo-Tibetan border trade? Lipulekh Pass.

Which highly revered yatra, held every 12 years in the Garhwal Himalayas, creates a massive, albeit temporary, economic boom for the local mountain communities along its 280 km trekking route? Nanda Devi Raj Jat Yatra.

What is the state's primary economic argument for demanding a "Green Bonus" from the Central Government? To compensate the state for conserving its massive forest cover (which provides ecological services to the nation) at the cost of its own industrial expansion.

Which local citrus fruit, exceptionally rich in Vitamin C and distinctively large, recently acquired a GI tag and is heavily processed for state-owned juice brands? Malta.

Which IT park in Dehradun was specifically developed by SIDCUL to attract major multinational software companies? IT Park, Sahastradhara Road.

Which state tax continues to be a major independent revenue generator for the Uttarakhand Government outside the purview of GST? State Excise Duty (primarily on liquor) and Stamp Duty/Registration Fees.

The promotion of "Tehri Lake" as a world-class destination primarily focuses on which specific sub-sector of tourism? Adventure and Water Sports Tourism.

To support female entrepreneurs, the Uttarakhand government has mandated a certain percentage of public procurement from women-owned Micro and Small Enterprises. What is this percentage? 3% (in line with central MSME guidelines, though local state implementations prioritize it heavily).

Which local grain, heavily used during fasting (Navratri) across India, is a major cash crop for high-altitude farmers in Uttarakhand? Kuttu (Buckwheat) / Amaranth (Ramdana).

The "Gauchar Mela" in Chamoli was historically started in 1943 by Deputy Commissioner Burnell for what primary economic purpose? To serve as an industrial and trade fair, especially connecting Bhotia traders from the upper Himalayas with plain merchants.

What is the "Uttarakhand Single Window Clearance System" portal primarily designed to do? To facilitate ease of doing business by providing a one-stop digital platform for investors to get all necessary state approvals and licenses.

In the context of Uttarakhand's budget, what is the significance of "Gender Budgeting"? An allocation strategy that evaluates the state budget's impact on women and ensures specific funds are dedicated to women's economic empowerment and welfare schemes.

🏔️ Uttarakhand State Economy: Micro-Economics & ODTP Scheme
What is the total length of the international border Uttarakhand shares with Nepal and China, which heavily influences its border trade economy? Approximately 620 km (345 km with China, 275 km with Nepal).

To promote local crafts and agriculture, Uttarakhand launched the "One District Two Products" (ODTP) scheme. What are the designated products for Almora? Tweed/Woolen craft and Bal Mithai.

Under the ODTP scheme, what are the designated products for Dehradun? Bakery products and Basmati rice.

What are the designated products for Haridwar under the ODTP scheme? Jaggery (Gud) and Honey.

What are the designated products for Uttarkashi under the ODTP scheme? Apple-based products and Woolen craft.

The "Pound and Uncal" weights were traditionally used for the Indo-Tibetan barter trade by which indigenous trading community of Uttarakhand? The Bhotia tribe.

Which massive upcoming railway link, featuring 12 planned stations, is expected to economically transform the Garhwal region? The Rishikesh-Karnaprayag Railway line.

What is the primary economic purpose of the "Ghansali-Bhilangana" micro-hydel projects? To provide local grid power and generate grassroots employment without large-scale ecological displacement.

Which state department manages the "Himadri" emporiums that sell local artisan goods? Uttarakhand Handloom and Handicraft Development Council (UHHDC).

The "Aroma Valley" project, designed to boost the perfume and essential oil industry, is being developed in which district? Udham Singh Nagar (Kashipur).

According to state indices, which Uttarakhand district ranks highest in the Human Development Index (HDI)? Dehradun.

Which district currently ranks lowest in the state's HDI? Tehri Garhwal.

What is the primary economic driver of the famous "Kaudiyala-Shivpuri" stretch along the Ganges? River rafting and adventure tourism.

Which Uttarakhand town is globally recognized as a major hub for the "Optics and Instruments" manufacturing industry? Dehradun (home to IRDE and Opto Electronics factory).

The "Gularbhoj" reservoir is currently being developed to boost which specific economic activity? Water sports tourism (Udham Singh Nagar).

The annual "Tehri Lake Festival" is organized primarily to promote what? Adventure and water sports tourism.

Which central scheme is utilized by Uttarakhand to develop specialized "Eco-Circuits" and "Heritage Circuits"? Swadesh Darshan Scheme.

The PRASHAD Scheme (Pilgrimage Rejuvenation and Spiritual Heritage Augmentation Drive) currently covers which major shrines in Uttarakhand? Kedarnath and Badrinath.

What is the main objective of the "Uttarakhand Sheep and Wool Development Board"? To improve the yield and quality of local wool by introducing Australian Merino crossbreeds.

"Ringal" craft, which holds a GI tag and supports rural livelihoods, is predominantly practiced in which districts? Chamoli and Rudraprayag.

Which Uttarakhand district is known as the state's "Copper City" (Tamra Nagari) and holds a GI tag for its traditional copper craft? Almora.

The "Harela" festival, deeply tied to the state's agrarian economy, marks the onset of which critical farming season? The Monsoon/Sowing season.

"Bhabar" grass (Eulaliopsis binata) is economically significant in Uttarakhand for manufacturing what? Ropes and paper.

What is the primary function of the Uttarakhand State Seed and Organic Production Certification Agency (USSOPCA)? To certify organic produce, allowing local farmers to command premium market pricing.

Which border village in Chamoli is being aggressively developed under the "Vibrant Villages Programme" to boost local economy and stem out-migration? Mana.

"Nelong Valley", an inner-line region recently opened for controlled tourism to boost the border economy, is located in which district? Uttarkashi.

What is the primary focus of the "Mukhyamantri Anchal Amrit Yojana"? Providing fortified milk to children in Anganwadi centers to combat malnutrition.

Which Uttarakhand district has the highest Credit-Deposit (CD) ratio, indicating high industrial lending and economic activity? Udham Singh Nagar.

📉 Advanced Economic Curves, Theories & Inflation
What is "Hyperinflation"? Extremely rapid, out-of-control inflation, often exceeding 50% per month, rendering the currency almost worthless.

What is "Creeping Inflation"? A mild, slow, and predictable increase in prices (usually 2-3% annually), generally considered healthy for economic growth.

What is the "Phillips Curve"? A graphical curve showing the historical inverse relationship between the rate of unemployment and the rate of inflation.

What is the "Laffer Curve"? A curve showing the relationship between tax rates and tax revenue, suggesting that there is an optimal tax rate that maximizes revenue.

Laffer Curve graph, AI generated
Shutterstock
What is the "Kuznets Curve"? A curve hypothesizing that as an economy develops, market forces first increase economic inequality, and then decrease it.

What is "Engel's Law"? An economic observation stating that as household income rises, the proportion of income spent on food falls, even if absolute expenditure on food rises.

What is "Gresham's Law"? A monetary principle stating that "bad money drives out good" (if there are two forms of commodity money in circulation, the more valuable one will be hoarded and disappear from circulation).

What is the "Twin Deficit Hypothesis"? A macroeconomic theory that a country's fiscal deficit and current account deficit are closely linked and often move together.

What is "Helicopter Money"? A hypothetical, unconventional monetary policy involving printing large sums of money and distributing it directly to the public to spur inflation and economic output.

What is "Capital Gains Tax"? A tax levied on the profit realized on the sale of a non-inventory asset, like stocks, bonds, or real estate.

What is the "Securities Transaction Tax" (STT)? A direct tax levied on every purchase or sale of securities listed on recognized stock exchanges in India.

Which route allows Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) into India without prior approval from the Government or the RBI? The Automatic Route.

Which government body was abolished and replaced by the Foreign Investment Facilitation Portal (FIFP) to clear FDI proposals? The Foreign Investment Promotion Board (FIPB).

What is a "Special Economic Zone" (SEZ)? A designated area in a country subject to different, more liberal economic regulations than other regions, designed to boost exports and attract FDI.

🌾 Agriculture, Banking & Demographics Wrap-up
Under the PM Fasal Bima Yojana, what is the uniform premium paid by farmers for all Kharif crops? 2%.

Under the PM Fasal Bima Yojana, what is the premium paid by farmers for all Rabi crops? 1.5%.

Under the PM Fasal Bima Yojana, what is the premium paid by farmers for annual commercial and horticultural crops? 5%.

What is the "Demographic Dividend"? The economic growth potential resulting from a shift in a population's age structure, where the working-age population is larger than the non-working-age population.

According to the 2011 Census, what is the defined age bracket for the working-age group in India? 15 to 59 years.

Which index is published by the UNDP to measure average achievement in key dimensions of human development? Human Development Index (HDI).

What are the three core dimensions of the HDI? A long and healthy life, access to knowledge, and a decent standard of living.

Who were the two primary economists who developed the Human Development Index? Mahbub ul Haq and Amartya Sen.

Which organization publishes the "World Economic Outlook" report? International Monetary Fund (IMF).

Which organization publishes the "Global Competitiveness Report"? World Economic Forum (WEF).

What is the "Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code" (IBC), 2016? A consolidated legal framework enacted to resolve insolvencies and bankruptcies of corporations, individuals, and partnerships in a time-bound manner.

Who is the regulatory body for the IBC in India? Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI).

For commercial banks in India, what is the standard Priority Sector Lending (PSL) target set by the RBI? 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit (ANBC).

📈 Financial Markets, Technology & Terminology
What does "BSE SENSEX" stand for? Bombay Stock Exchange Sensitive Index (comprising the top 30 companies).

What does "NIFTY 50" stand for? National Stock Exchange Fifty (comprising the top 50 companies).

What is "Insider Trading"? The illegal practice of trading on the stock exchange to one's own advantage by using confidential, non-public information.

What is a "Mutual Fund"? A professionally managed investment vehicle funded by shareholders that pools money to trade in diversified holdings like stocks and bonds.

What does SEBI stand for? Securities and Exchange Board of India.

In which year was SEBI given statutory powers to regulate the market? 1992 (through the SEBI Act).

What is a "Fiat Currency"? A national currency that is not pegged to the price of a physical commodity (like gold) but is backed entirely by the government's decree.

What is "Cryptocurrency"? A digital or virtual currency secured by cryptography and typically based on decentralized blockchain technology.

What is the "SWIFT" network? Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication—a vast messaging network used by banks to securely transmit information and instructions globally.

What does NEFT stand for in the Indian banking system? National Electronic Funds Transfer.

What does RTGS stand for? Real-Time Gross Settlement.

What is the minimum amount that can be remitted through RTGS? Rs. 2 Lakhs.

What does NPCI stand for? National Payments Corporation of India.

What does UPI stand for? Unified Payments Interface.

What is the "MUDRA" Bank? Micro Units Development and Refinance Agency Bank—set up to provide funding to non-corporate, non-farm sector income-generating activities.

What are the three specific loan categories under the MUDRA scheme? Shishu, Kishore, and Tarun.

What is the loan limit for a "Shishu" loan under MUDRA? Up to Rs. 50,000.

What is the loan limit for a "Kishore" loan under MUDRA? From Rs. 50,001 to Rs. 5,00,000.

What is the loan limit for a "Tarun" loan under MUDRA? From Rs. 5,00,001 to Rs. 10,00,000.

What is a "Gilt-edged" market? The market for trading government securities, known for being highly secure and risk-free.

What is "Hawala"? An informal and illegal method of transferring money without any physical money actually moving, based entirely on a network of brokers honoring trust.

What is the "Bank Rate"? The rate at which the RBI is ready to buy or rediscount bills of exchange or other commercial papers.

What is an "Escrow Account"? A temporary pass-through account held by a third party during the process of a transaction between two parties.

What is a "Hedge Fund"? A pooled investment fund that trades in relatively liquid assets and uses highly complex trading, portfolio-construction, and risk management techniques to improve performance.

What is "Monopolistic Competition"? A market structure in which many firms sell products that are similar but not identical (e.g., the restaurant or clothing industry).

What does the term "Laissez-faire" mean in economics? An economic environment in which transactions between private parties are free from any form of government intervention such as regulation, privileges, tariffs, and subsidies.

What is "Microeconomics"? The branch of economics that studies the behavior of individuals and firms in making decisions regarding the allocation of scarce resources.

What is "Macroeconomics"? The branch of economics that studies the behavior and performance of an economy as a whole (e.g., inflation, GDP, unemployment).

What is the "Purchasing Power Parity" (PPP)? An economic theory that allows the comparison of the purchasing power of various world currencies to one another.

By Purchasing Power Parity (PPP), what is India's global rank in terms of GDP? 3rd (after China and the USA).

What is "Foreign Exchange Reserves" (Forex)? Assets held on reserve by a central bank in foreign currencies, which can include bonds, treasury bills, and other government securities.

What is "Venture Capital"? A form of private equity and a type of financing that investors provide to startup companies and small businesses that are believed to have long-term growth potential.

What is "Angel Investment"? An investment by a high-net-worth individual who provides financial backing for small startups or entrepreneurs, typically in exchange for ownership equity.

What does "Amortization" mean in finance? The process of spreading out a loan into a series of fixed payments over time.

What is "Arbitrage"? The simultaneous purchase and sale of an asset to profit from an imbalance in the price.

What is a "Cartel"? An association of manufacturers or suppliers with the purpose of maintaining prices at a high level and restricting competition (e.g., OPEC).

What is "Elasticity of Demand"? A measure of how much the quantity demanded of a good responds to a change in its price.

What is a "Sovereign Wealth Fund"? A state-owned investment fund that invests in real and financial assets such as stocks, bonds, real estate, or precious metals.

What is the "Base Year" effect? The impact of the choice of base year on the calculation of economic data, such as inflation or GDP growth.

What is the "Shadow Economy"? Illicit economic activity (such as black market transactions and undeclared work) existing alongside a country's official economy.

What is "Financial Inclusion"? The process of ensuring access to financial services and timely and adequate credit where needed by vulnerable groups such as weaker sections and low-income groups at an affordable cost.

What is a "Start-up"? A company in the first stage of its operations, typically founded by one or more entrepreneurs who want to develop a product or service for which they believe there is demand.

What is "E-commerce"? The buying and selling of goods and services, or the transmitting of funds or data, over an electronic network, primarily the internet.

What is "Globalization"? The process by which businesses or other organizations develop international influence or start operating on an international scale.

What is "Sustainable Development"? Economic development that is conducted without depletion of natural resources, meeting the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.

Environment & Ecology has become one of the most heavily weighted sections in recent civil services exams, and General Science forms the bedrock of everyday administrative understanding.

🌿 Ecology & Ecosystem Fundamentals
Who coined the term "Ecology"? Ernst Haeckel (in 1866).

Who coined the term "Ecosystem"? Arthur Tansley (in 1935).

What is the functional unit of nature where living organisms interact among themselves and with the surrounding physical environment? An Ecosystem.

What is the term for the specific physical space occupied by an organism? Habitat.

What is the term for the functional role or specific position of a species within its ecosystem? Ecological Niche.

No two species can occupy the exact same ecological niche in the same habitat for long. What is this principle called? Gause's Competitive Exclusion Principle.

What is the transitional zone between two different ecosystems (like a marsh between a river and land) called? Ecotone.

Species that have a disproportionately large effect on their natural environment relative to their abundance are known as what? Keystone Species.

What is the term for species that serve as an early warning of environmental degradation (like lichens for air quality)? Indicator Species.

In a food chain, what occupies the first trophic level? Producers (Autotrophs, like green plants).

Herbivores that eat producers are classified as what? Primary Consumers.

ecological food chain and food web diagram, AI generated
Shutterstock
Explore
Who proposed the "10% Law" of energy transfer in a food chain? Raymond Lindeman.

According to the 10% Law, what happens to the remaining 90% of energy at each trophic level? It is lost to the environment as heat during respiration and metabolic processes.

Because energy is lost at each step, an ecological pyramid of energy is always shaped how? Always upright.

What is the progressive increase in the concentration of toxic substances (like DDT) at successive higher trophic levels called? Biomagnification.

What is a symbiotic relationship where both species benefit called? Mutualism (e.g., pollination by bees).

What is a symbiotic relationship where one species benefits and the other is neither helped nor harmed? Commensalism (e.g., orchids growing on a tree).

What is a relationship where one organism benefits at the expense of another? Parasitism.

What is the process by which a bare, lifeless area is gradually colonized by biological communities over time? Ecological Succession.

What are the first organisms to colonize a bare rock or newly formed volcanic island called? Pioneer Species (e.g., lichens and mosses).

🔄 Biogeochemical Cycles & Pollution
Through which biological process is carbon removed from the atmosphere and fixed into organic compounds? Photosynthesis.

What is the primary natural process that releases carbon dioxide back into the atmosphere? Cellular Respiration.

Which is the most abundant greenhouse gas in the Earth's atmosphere? Water Vapor (though Carbon Dioxide is the most significant anthropogenic or human-made one).

Which greenhouse gas is primarily released from rice paddies, wetlands, and the digestive tracts of cattle? Methane (CH4).

What is the process of converting atmospheric nitrogen gas into a usable form (ammonia or nitrates) by bacteria called? Nitrogen Fixation.

nitrogen cycle diagram, AI generated
Shutterstock
Explore
Which symbiotic bacteria found in the root nodules of leguminous plants is famous for fixing nitrogen? Rhizobium.

What is the phenomenon where a water body becomes overly enriched with nutrients (nitrates and phosphates), leading to algal blooms and oxygen depletion? Eutrophication.

What measures the amount of dissolved oxygen needed by aerobic biological organisms to break down organic material in a water sample? Biological Oxygen Demand (BOD).

Does a high BOD indicate clean water or highly polluted water? Highly polluted water.

Which heavy metal is responsible for the neurological disease known as "Minamata disease"? Mercury.

Which heavy metal poisoning causes "Itai-Itai" disease, leading to severe bone pain? Cadmium.

"Blue Baby Syndrome" (Methemoglobinemia) in infants is caused by the contamination of drinking water with what? Nitrates.

🧬 General Science: Biology Basics
Who discovered the cell in 1665? Robert Hooke.

Who is known as the "Father of Microbiology" for first observing living cells under a microscope? Antonie van Leeuwenhoek.

Which cell organelle is famously known as the "Powerhouse of the Cell"? Mitochondria.

mitochondria cell structure diagram, AI generated
Shutterstock
Which cell organelle is known as the "Suicide Bag" because it contains digestive enzymes? Lysosome.

Where are proteins synthesized within the cell? Ribosomes.

What is the control center of the eukaryotic cell that contains the genetic material (DNA)? Nucleus.

Which component is present in plant cells but completely absent in animal cells? Cell Wall (and Chloroplasts).

Who is known as the "Father of Genetics"? Gregor Mendel (based on his work with pea plants).

What is the full form of DNA? Deoxyribonucleic Acid.

Who discovered the double-helix structure of DNA? James Watson and Francis Crick (using data from Rosalind Franklin).

Which blood vessels carry oxygenated blood away from the heart to the body? Arteries.

Which blood vessels carry deoxygenated blood from the body back to the heart? Veins.

human heart anatomy and blood flow, AI generated
Shutterstock
Explore
What is the only artery in the human body that carries deoxygenated blood? The Pulmonary Artery.

What is the primary function of Red Blood Cells (RBCs)? To transport oxygen using the protein hemoglobin.

What is the primary function of White Blood Cells (WBCs)? To fight infection and provide immunity.

Which component of blood is responsible for blood clotting? Platelets.

Which blood group is known as the "Universal Donor"? O Negative (O-).

Which blood group is known as the "Universal Recipient"? AB Positive (AB+).

What is the largest gland in the human body? The Liver.

Which organ produces insulin to regulate blood sugar levels? The Pancreas.

What is the functional unit of the human kidney responsible for filtering blood? The Nephron.

Which part of the human brain controls involuntary actions like heartbeat and breathing? The Medulla Oblongata.

Which part of the brain is responsible for maintaining posture, balance, and voluntary muscle coordination? The Cerebellum.

🧪 General Science: Chemistry Essentials
Who proposed the modern atomic theory? John Dalton.

What are the three fundamental subatomic particles? Protons, Neutrons, and Electrons.

Who discovered the electron? J.J. Thomson.

Who discovered the proton? Ernest Rutherford.

Who discovered the neutron? James Chadwick.

The atomic number of an element is equal to the number of what? Protons in its nucleus.

What are isotopes? Atoms of the same element with the same number of protons but a different number of neutrons (different mass number).

Who is known as the "Father of the Periodic Table"? Dmitri Mendeleev.

What is the most abundant element in the universe? Hydrogen.

What is the pH value of pure, neutral water? 7.

pH scale showing acids and bases, AI generated
Shutterstock
A pH value less than 7 indicates what type of solution? Acidic.

A pH value greater than 7 indicates what type of solution? Basic (Alkaline).

Which acid is naturally found in our stomach to aid digestion? Hydrochloric Acid (HCl).

Which acid is found in ant and bee stings? Formic Acid (Methanoic acid).

What is the common chemical name for Baking Soda? Sodium Bicarbonate.

What is the common chemical name for Washing Soda? Sodium Carbonate.

What is the common chemical name for Table Salt? Sodium Chloride.

What is the chemical name of the compound commonly known as "Laughing Gas"? Nitrous Oxide.

What is "Dry Ice"? Solid Carbon Dioxide.

Which metal remains a liquid at standard room temperature? Mercury.

Which non-metal remains a liquid at standard room temperature? Bromine.

What is the hardest naturally occurring substance on Earth? Diamond (an allotrope of Carbon).

Which process is used to separate the components of petroleum (crude oil)? Fractional Distillation.

What is the process of coating iron with a layer of zinc to prevent it from rusting? Galvanization.

Brass is an alloy of which two metals? Copper and Zinc.

⚛️ General Science: Physics Fundamentals
What is the SI unit of Force? Newton.

What is the SI unit of Work and Energy? Joule.

What is the SI unit of Power? Watt.

What is the SI unit of Electrical Resistance? Ohm.

What is the SI unit of Electric Current? Ampere.

According to Newton's First Law of Motion, an object at rest stays at rest unless acted upon by what? An unbalanced external force (Law of Inertia).

Which law of motion states that for every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction? Newton's Third Law of Motion.

What is the acceleration due to gravity on the surface of the Earth? Approximately 9.8 meters per second squared.

What happens to the weight of an object if it is taken to the Moon? It decreases (because the Moon's gravity is 1/6th of Earth's), but its mass remains constant.

What is the escape velocity from the Earth's surface? 11.2 km/s.

Sound waves are which type of wave? Longitudinal mechanical waves.

Can sound travel through a vacuum? No, it requires a material medium (solid, liquid, or gas).

Light waves are which type of wave? Transverse electromagnetic waves.

electromagnetic spectrum diagram, AI generated
Shutterstock
Can light travel through a vacuum? Yes.

What is the exact speed of light in a vacuum? Approximately 300,000 kilometers per second (3×10 
8
  m/s).

Which optical phenomenon causes a pencil submerged in a glass of water to appear bent? Refraction.

Which optical phenomenon is responsible for the formation of a rainbow? Dispersion, refraction, and total internal reflection of sunlight by water droplets.

Which optical phenomenon explains why the clear sky appears blue? Scattering of light (Rayleigh scattering).

What type of mirror is commonly used by dentists to see a magnified image of teeth? Concave mirror.

What type of mirror is used as a rear-view mirror in vehicles because it provides a wider field of view? Convex mirror.

🐅 Biodiversity & Conservation
Who introduced the concept of "Biodiversity Hotspots" in 1988? Norman Myers.

What are the two strict criteria for a region to qualify as a biodiversity hotspot? It must contain at least 1,500 species of vascular plants as endemics, and it must have lost at least 70% of its original native habitat.

How many biodiversity hotspots are entirely or partially located in India? Four (The Himalayas, Indo-Burma, Western Ghats & Sri Lanka, and Sundaland/Nicobar group).

What is "In-situ" conservation? The conservation of species in their natural habitats (e.g., National Parks, Wildlife Sanctuaries, Biosphere Reserves).

What is "Ex-situ" conservation? The conservation of components of biological diversity outside their natural habitats (e.g., Zoos, Botanical Gardens, Seed Banks).

Which international organization maintains the "Red List of Threatened Species"? The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN).

Where is the headquarters of the IUCN located? Gland, Switzerland.

In the IUCN Red List, what category is given to a species facing an extremely high risk of extinction in the wild? Critically Endangered (CR).

Which iconic animal is the official symbol of the World Wide Fund for Nature (WWF)? The Giant Panda.

In which year was "Project Tiger" launched by the Government of India to protect the Bengal Tiger? 1973.

In which year was "Project Elephant" launched? 1992.

Which national park in India is famous for successfully conserving the critically endangered Sangai (brow-antlered deer)? Keibul Lamjao National Park (Manipur).

Which region in Gujarat is the last remaining natural habitat of the Asiatic Lion? Gir National Park.

Which marine reptile is famous for its mass nesting phenomenon called "Arribada" on the coast of Odisha? The Olive Ridley Turtle.

What is the name of the designated mass nesting site for Olive Ridley turtles in Odisha? Gahirmatha Beach.

Which Indian state is recognized as the "Falcon Capital of the World" due to the massive roosting of Amur Falcons? Nagaland.

What is the state animal of Uttarakhand, known for its distinct musk pod? The Alpine Musk Deer.

The Jim Corbett National Park, India's first national park, was originally established in 1936 under what name? Hailey National Park.

What are "Sacred Groves"? Tracts of forest set aside by local communities, protected by strict religious beliefs and traditional taboos (e.g., Kavus of Kerala, Devrais of Maharashtra).

Which coral reef system in India is situated off the coast of Tamil Nadu and is a designated Biosphere Reserve? The Gulf of Mannar.

🌍 Climate Change & International Treaties
What is the "Greenhouse Effect"? The process by which radiation from a planet's atmosphere warms the planet's surface to a temperature above what it would be without its atmosphere.

greenhouse effect diagram, AI generated
Shutterstock
Which specific treaty was adopted in 1997 to commit state parties to reduce greenhouse gas emissions based on the premise that global warming exists? The Kyoto Protocol.

Under the Kyoto Protocol, what was the mechanism that allowed developed countries to invest in emission-reduction projects in developing countries? The Clean Development Mechanism (CDM).

Which landmark international treaty was adopted in 2015 to limit global warming to well below 2 degrees Celsius, preferably to 1.5 degrees Celsius? The Paris Agreement.

What is the layer of the stratosphere that absorbs most of the Sun's ultraviolet (UV) radiation called? The Ozone Layer.

Which chemical compounds were primarily responsible for the depletion of the ozone layer? Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs).

Which international treaty was signed in 1987 to phase out the production of numerous substances that are responsible for ozone depletion? The Montreal Protocol.

Which amendment to the Montreal Protocol aims to phase down hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs), which are potent greenhouse gases? The Kigali Amendment.

Which international convention was signed in 1971 in Iran to specifically protect and conserve wetlands of international importance? The Ramsar Convention.

What is the "Montreux Record"? A register of Ramsar wetland sites where changes in ecological character have occurred, are occurring, or are likely to occur due to technological developments, pollution, or other human interference.

Which Indian lake was removed from the Montreux Record in 2002 after successful ecological restoration? Chilika Lake (Odisha).

Which two Indian sites are currently listed on the Montreux Record? Keoladeo National Park (Rajasthan) and Loktak Lake (Manipur).

What is CITES? Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (aims to ensure international trade in wild animals and plants does not threaten their survival).

Which convention is known as the Bonn Convention, aiming to conserve terrestrial, marine, and avian migratory species throughout their range? Convention on Migratory Species (CMS).

The Basel Convention regulates the transboundary movements of what? Hazardous wastes and their disposal.

The Stockholm Convention is a global treaty to protect human health and the environment from what specific type of chemicals? Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs).

The Minamata Convention is an international treaty designed to protect human health from the adverse effects of which toxic heavy metal? Mercury.

Where is the secretariat of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) located? Bonn, Germany.

Which Indian organization conducts the biennial assessment of the country's forest cover, publishing the India State of Forest Report (ISFR)? The Forest Survey of India (FSI).

What is the term for the process of capturing and storing atmospheric carbon dioxide to mitigate global warming? Carbon Sequestration.

🧬 Biology: Human Health, Vitamins & Diseases
Which vitamin is synthesized by the human body when the skin is exposed to sunlight? Vitamin D.

A deficiency of Vitamin C (Ascorbic Acid) causes which disease characterized by bleeding gums? Scurvy.

A deficiency of Vitamin A (Retinol) primarily affects which human organ, causing Night Blindness? The Eyes.

Which vitamin is essential for the coagulation (clotting) of blood? Vitamin K.

Which disease is caused by a severe deficiency of protein in the diet of infants, leading to a swollen belly? Kwashiorkor.

What is the vector (carrier) for the deadly disease Malaria? The female Anopheles mosquito.

What type of pathogen actually causes Malaria? A protozoan parasite of the genus Plasmodium.

Which mosquito is the primary vector for Dengue, Chikungunya, and Zika viruses? The Aedes aegypti mosquito.

Tuberculosis (TB) is a contagious infection that primarily attacks the lungs. Is it caused by a virus or a bacterium? A bacterium (Mycobacterium tuberculosis).

Which vaccine is administered immediately after birth to protect infants against Tuberculosis? BCG (Bacillus Calmette-Guérin) vaccine.

Which human gland is often referred to as the "Master Gland" because it controls the functions of many other endocrine glands? The Pituitary Gland.

Which hormone is released by the adrenal glands during high-stress situations, triggering a "fight-or-flight" response? Adrenaline (Epinephrine).

Lack of iodine in the diet causes the enlargement of which gland, resulting in a Goiter? The Thyroid Gland.

Which infectious viral disease was officially declared eradicated globally by the WHO in 1980? Smallpox.

AIDS (Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome) is caused by the HIV virus. Which specific immune cells does HIV target and destroy? CD4 T-cells (White blood cells).

What is the function of the Xylem tissue in plants? To transport water and dissolved minerals from the roots to the rest of the plant.

plant tissue xylem and phloem, AI generated
Shutterstock
What is the function of the Phloem tissue in plants? To transport sugars (food) synthesized during photosynthesis from the leaves to other parts of the plant.

What are the tiny pores on the surface of plant leaves responsible for gas exchange called? Stomata.

Which green pigment in plants is essential for capturing solar energy during photosynthesis? Chlorophyll.

Which metal ion is found at the very center of the chlorophyll molecule? Magnesium.

🔭 General Science: Physics Concepts & Instruments
Which scientific instrument is used to measure atmospheric pressure? Barometer.

Which instrument is used by doctors to measure human blood pressure? Sphygmomanometer.

Which instrument is used to measure the purity of milk (its specific gravity)? Lactometer.

Which instrument is used to measure the intensity of earthquakes? Seismograph.

Which instrument is used to measure high temperatures (like inside a furnace)? Pyrometer.

What is the phenomenon where a changing magnetic field induces an electric current in a conductor? Electromagnetic Induction.

Who discovered the principle of Electromagnetic Induction, paving the way for modern electric generators? Michael Faraday.

What is the energy possessed by an object due to its motion called? Kinetic Energy.

What is the energy possessed by an object due to its position or state called? Potential Energy.

According to the Law of Conservation of Energy, energy can neither be created nor destroyed; it can only be what? Transformed from one form to another.

Which subatomic force is responsible for holding the protons and neutrons together inside the atomic nucleus? The Strong Nuclear Force.

What is the process in which a heavy atomic nucleus (like Uranium-235) splits into two lighter nuclei, releasing a massive amount of energy? Nuclear Fission.

nuclear fission vs fusion process, AI generated
Shutterstock
Explore
What is the process in which two light atomic nuclei combine to form a heavier nucleus, releasing energy? Nuclear Fusion.

Which process powers the Sun and all other stars—nuclear fission or nuclear fusion? Nuclear Fusion.

Which device converts mechanical energy into electrical energy? A Generator (or Dynamo).

Which device converts electrical energy into mechanical energy? An Electric Motor.

What type of wave is used in Sonar technology to detect objects underwater? Ultrasonic waves (high-frequency sound waves).

What type of electromagnetic radiation is heavily used in medical imaging to view the structure of bones? X-rays.

Who discovered X-rays in 1895? Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen.

What is the SI unit of electric charge? Coulomb.

🌱 Environmental Pollution & Miscellaneous Science
Which chemical compound is known as "Tear Gas," commonly used for riot control? CS gas (o-chlorobenzylidenemalononitrile) or Chloroacetophenone.

Photochemical smog is primarily composed of ground-level ozone, nitrogen dioxide, and what other volatile organic compounds? Peroxyacetyl Nitrate (PAN).

Which scale measures the concentration of hydrogen ions in a solution? The pH scale.

Acid rain is primarily caused by the emissions of which two acidic gases from factories and vehicles? Sulfur Dioxide (SO 
2
​
 ) and Nitrogen Oxides (NO 
x
​
 ).

What is the pure, solid form of carbon left over after coal is heated in the absence of air? Coke.

Which gas is most commonly used in fire extinguishers because it does not support combustion and is heavier than air? Carbon Dioxide (CO 
2
​
 ).

What is the primary constituent of natural gas and biogas? Methane.

What is the primary constituent of Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG) used in household cylinders? Butane and Propane.

Since LPG is highly flammable but naturally odorless, which chemical is added to it to give it a strong smell to detect leaks? Ethyl Mercaptan.

What is the chemical name for Vitamin B12, a crucial vitamin for nerve function and blood cell formation? Cobalamin (it contains the metal Cobalt).

What is the phenomenon responsible for the spherical shape of liquid drops? Surface Tension.

Why does ice float on water? Because ice is less dense than liquid water (due to its crystalline structure holding water molecules further apart).

What is the biological process of turning sugar into alcohol and carbon dioxide by yeast? Fermentation.

Who discovered the process of pasteurization (heating milk or wine to kill bacteria)? Louis Pasteur.

Who discovered the first antibiotic, Penicillin, in 1928? Alexander Fleming.

Which radioactive isotope of carbon is heavily used in archaeology to determine the age of ancient organic materials? Carbon-14.

What is the process by which solid ice turns directly into a gas without passing through the liquid phase? Sublimation (e.g., Dry ice, Camphor).

Which component of the atmosphere protects Earth from small meteors burning up upon entry? The Mesosphere.

What is the speed of sound in dry air at room temperature? Approximately 343 meters per second.

Who formulated the three laws of planetary motion, describing how planets orbit the Sun? Johannes Kepler.

🚀 Space Technology & Astronomy
In which year was the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) formally established? 1969.

What was the name of India's first satellite, launched in 1975? Aryabhata.

Where is the headquarters of ISRO located? Bengaluru.

What does PSLV, ISRO's highly successful "workhorse" rocket, stand for? Polar Satellite Launch Vehicle.

What does GSLV, used for launching heavier payloads, stand for? Geosynchronous Satellite Launch Vehicle.

The advanced upper stage of a GSLV rocket uses a "Cryogenic" engine. What extreme-cold propellants does it use? Liquid Hydrogen (fuel) and Liquid Oxygen (oxidizer).

On what exact date did India's Chandrayaan-3 successfully land on the Moon? August 23, 2023.

What name was officially given to the lunar landing site of the Chandrayaan-3 Vikram lander? Shiv Shakti Point.

What is the name of India's first dedicated space mission to study the Sun? Aditya-L1.

In astronomy, what exactly is a "Light Year"? The total distance that light travels in a vacuum in one Earth year.

Which is the largest planet in our solar system? Jupiter.

Which is the hottest planet in our solar system due to a runaway greenhouse effect? Venus.

What is the nearest star to Earth, other than the Sun? Proxima Centauri.

What is the theoretical boundary around a black hole beyond which absolutely nothing, not even light, can escape? The Event Horizon.

What is the official name of ISRO's upcoming flagship human spaceflight mission? Gaganyaan.

What is the name of India's first dedicated multi-wavelength space observatory? Astrosat.

What is the name of NASA's premier infrared space telescope, the scientific successor to the Hubble, launched in 2021? James Webb Space Telescope (JWST).

When a satellite is in an elliptical orbit around the Earth, what is the point closest to the Earth called? Perigee.

What is the point in orbit that is farthest from the Earth called? Apogee.

What is the name of the independent regional navigation satellite system developed by India (equivalent to GPS)? NavIC (Navigation with Indian Constellation).

How many satellites currently make up the active NavIC constellation? Seven (7).

What is the name of India's primary spaceport located in Sriharikota, Andhra Pradesh? Satish Dhawan Space Centre.

Which astronomical unit of distance is equivalent to approximately 3.26 light-years? Parsec.

Which major spiral galaxy is the closest neighbor to our Milky Way? The Andromeda Galaxy.

What astronomical phenomenon causes the tails of comets to always point away from the Sun? The Solar Wind.

🌳 Advanced Ecology & Environmental Legislations
In which year was the landmark Wildlife (Protection) Act enacted in India? 1972.

In which year was the Environment (Protection) Act (EPA) enacted? 1986.

The Environment (Protection) Act of 1986 was enacted largely in the wake of which major industrial disaster? The Bhopal Gas Tragedy (1984).

Which highly toxic gas leaked from the Union Carbide plant during the Bhopal Gas Tragedy? Methyl Isocyanate (MIC).

In which year was the Forest (Conservation) Act enacted to strictly regulate the diversion of forest land for non-forest purposes? 1980.

In which year was the Biological Diversity Act enacted in India? 2002.

Which specialized environmental court was established in 2010 to handle cases relating to environmental protection and forest conservation? The National Green Tribunal (NGT).

Where is the Principal Bench (headquarters) of the National Green Tribunal located? New Delhi.

What is the specific use of microorganisms or plants to clean up polluted environments (like oil spills) called? Bioremediation.

When ocean water becomes too warm, corals expel the symbiotic algae living in their tissues, turning completely white. What is this called? Coral Bleaching.

What is the primary cause of "Ocean Acidification"? The increased absorption of excess atmospheric Carbon Dioxide (CO 
2
​
 ) by seawater.

What term refers to the total amount of greenhouse gases generated by our actions and daily activities? Carbon Footprint.

On which date is the International Day for Biological Diversity celebrated globally? May 22.

On which date is World Environment Day celebrated? June 5.

On which date is World Ozone Day celebrated? September 16.

National Science Day is celebrated in India on February 28 to mark which historic discovery? The Raman Effect (by Sir C.V. Raman).

Species that are found uniquely in one specific geographic area and nowhere else in the world are called what? Endemic Species.

What is the variety of genes and inheritable traits within a single species called? Genetic Diversity.

What is a non-native species that spreads aggressively outside its natural range, causing harm to the local ecosystem, called? An Invasive Alien Species.

Name one of the most notorious invasive aquatic weeds choking water bodies across India. Water Hyacinth (locally known as "Terror of Bengal").

What is the sustainable land-use system where trees are grown around or among crops or pastureland called? Agroforestry.

What is the natural or artificial aging of a lake characterized by excessive plant and algal growth due to nutrient enrichment? Eutrophication.

The Global Environment Facility (GEF) serves as a financial mechanism for multiple conventions. Can you name two? UNFCCC (Climate Change) and CBD (Biological Diversity).

Which international protocol under the CBD deals with the "fair and equitable sharing of benefits" arising from the use of genetic resources? The Nagoya Protocol.

Which international protocol deals specifically with "biosafety" and the safe handling of Genetically Modified Organisms (GMOs)? The Cartagena Protocol.

🔬 General Science: Chemistry & Physics in Everyday Life
Why does oil constantly rise up the wick of an oil lamp? Due to Capillary Action (driven by surface tension).

Mirages seen in hot deserts are an optical illusion caused by which phenomenon of light? Total Internal Reflection.

Optical fibers, used for high-speed internet, transmit data using which physical principle? Total Internal Reflection.

Why do falling raindrops naturally assume a spherical shape? Due to Surface Tension (which minimizes surface area).

Which instrument is used to measure the electric current flowing in a circuit? Ammeter.

Which instrument is used to measure the potential difference (voltage) across two points in a circuit? Voltmeter.

Which synthetic polymer is universally used to make non-stick coatings for cookware? Teflon (Polytetrafluoroethylene).

The process of "Vulcanization" strengthens natural rubber by heating it with which chemical element? Sulfur.

What is the primary chemical component of standard glass? Silica (Silicon Dioxide).

Which organic acid is naturally present in sour milk and yogurt (curd)? Lactic Acid.

Which acid is naturally abundant in citrus fruits like lemons and oranges? Citric Acid.

What is the primary acid present in common kitchen Vinegar? Acetic Acid.

Which acid gives apples their characteristic tart taste? Malic Acid.

Who is credited with the serendipitous discovery of radioactivity in 1896? Henri Becquerel.

Which two highly radioactive elements were famously discovered by Marie and Pierre Curie? Radium and Polonium.

In radioactive decay, which type of particle is essentially a Helium nucleus (two protons and two neutrons)? Alpha particle.

Among Alpha, Beta, and Gamma radiation, which has the highest penetrating power? Gamma rays.

Heavy water is widely used as a moderator in nuclear power reactors. What is its chemical name? Deuterium Oxide (D 
2
​
 O).

What is the process of applying a protective zinc coating to steel or iron to prevent rusting? Galvanization.

Bronze is an alloy primarily composed of which two metals? Copper and Tin.

Solder, an alloy used to join electrical wires, is primarily made of which two metals? Lead and Tin.

Stainless steel is an alloy of iron, carbon, and which key element that makes it rust-resistant? Chromium.

The glowing filament of a traditional incandescent electric bulb is made of which metal? Tungsten (due to its extremely high melting point).

The internal heating element of appliances like electric irons and toasters is made of which alloy? Nichrome.

Which inert gas is commonly filled inside electric bulbs to prevent the tungsten filament from oxidizing and burning out? Argon.

🦴 General Science: Human Body, Genetics & Diseases
What is the hardest substance found in the human body? Tooth Enamel.

The fully developed skeletal system of an adult human consists of exactly how many bones? 206.

Which is the longest, heaviest, and strongest bone in the human body? The Femur (thigh bone).

Which is the smallest bone in the human body, located in the middle ear? The Stapes.

What is the tough band of fibrous connective tissue that connects muscles to bones? Tendon.

What is the tough connective tissue that connects bones to other bones at a joint? Ligament.

The enzyme "Amylase," present in human saliva, begins the digestion of which nutrient in the mouth? Starch (Carbohydrates).

Which powerful enzyme, secreted in the stomach, is responsible for breaking down proteins? Pepsin.

Which organ continuously produces "Bile," a fluid that helps digest fats? The Liver.

Where is bile temporarily stored and concentrated before it is released into the small intestine? The Gallbladder.

What is the fundamental, electrically excitable unit of the human nervous system? The Neuron (Nerve cell).

What is the microscopic gap between two neurons, across which neurotransmitters transmit chemical signals, called? A Synapse.

Which vital human blood cells uniquely lack a nucleus when they mature, to make more room for oxygen? Red Blood Cells (RBCs).

Which famous genetic disorder is caused by the presence of a complete or partial extra copy of chromosome 21? Down Syndrome.

Which sex-linked genetic disorder impairs the body's ability to make blood clots, leading to excessive bleeding? Hemophilia.

What is the primary biological pigment responsible for the color of human skin, hair, and eyes? Melanin.

Which colored part of the human eye controls the size of the pupil and, therefore, the amount of light entering it? The Iris.

What kind of image is physically formed on the retina of the human eye? Real and Inverted.

Myopia (short-sightedness) is optically corrected using spectacles with which type of lens? Concave Lens.

Hypermetropia (long-sightedness) is optically corrected using which type of lens? Convex Lens.

What is the scientific study of the interactions between living organisms and their physical environment? Ecology.

What is the microscopic study of the tissues of plants and animals called? Histology.

What is the branch of biology dedicated exclusively to the study of fungi? Mycology.

What is the branch of science concerned with the classification, naming, and grouping of biological organisms? Taxonomy.

Which 18th-century Swedish botanist is universally recognized as the "Father of Taxonomy"? Carl Linnaeus.

🧬 Biotechnology, Genetics & Immunology
What was the name of the first mammal successfully cloned from an adult somatic cell in 1996? Dolly the sheep.

Which revolutionary genetic engineering technique, often referred to as "molecular scissors," is used to precisely edit DNA? CRISPR-Cas9.

CRISPR Cas9 DNA editing, AI generated
Shutterstock
What does the acronym PCR stand for, a technique widely used to rapidly make millions of copies of a specific DNA sample? Polymerase Chain Reaction.

Which is the only Genetically Modified (GM) crop currently approved for commercial cultivation in India? Bt Cotton.

What does the "Bt" in Bt Cotton stand for? Bacillus thuringiensis (a soil bacterium that produces a toxin lethal to certain pests).

Which statutory body in India is responsible for the approval of proposals relating to the release of genetically engineered organisms and products? The Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC).

What are biological cells that can differentiate into any type of cell in the body and divide indefinitely called? Pluripotent Stem Cells.

Who is universally recognized as the "Father of Immunology" for developing the first successful smallpox vaccine? Edward Jenner.

When a foreign substance enters the body, the immune system produces specific Y-shaped proteins to neutralize it. What are they called? Antibodies.

What is the foreign substance (like a virus or bacterium) that triggers this immune response called? An Antigen.

Which type of white blood cell is primarily responsible for producing antibodies? B-cells (B-lymphocytes).

What kind of immunity is acquired when a person receives antibodies directly from another source (like a mother's breast milk) rather than producing them? Passive Immunity.

Which diagnostic test is used as the primary screening tool for detecting HIV infection? ELISA (Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay).

Which highly accurate test is generally used to confirm a positive ELISA result for HIV? The Western Blot test.

🦠 Human Diseases & Pathogens
Which insect acts as the vector for "Kala-azar" (Visceral Leishmaniasis)? The Sandfly.

Which insect acts as the vector for African Sleeping Sickness? The Tsetse fly.

Typhoid fever is a waterborne disease caused by which specific bacterium? Salmonella typhi.

Which diagnostic blood test is traditionally used to confirm Typhoid fever? The Widal Test.

Cholera, characterized by severe diarrhea and dehydration, is caused by which bacterium? Vibrio cholerae.

What are the three essential components of Oral Rehydration Solution (ORS) used to treat severe dehydration? Clean water, sugar, and salt.

A deficiency of Vitamin B1 (Thiamine) leads to which disease, affecting the nervous and cardiovascular systems? Beriberi.

A severe deficiency of Vitamin B3 (Niacin) leads to which disease, characterized by the "3 Ds": diarrhea, dermatitis, and dementia? Pellagra.

Prolonged consumption of groundwater contaminated with Arsenic leads to which severe vascular disease? Blackfoot Disease.

Inhalation of fine crystalline silica dust, often affecting miners and glass workers, causes which incurable lung disease? Silicosis.

What is the medical term for "Coal Worker's Pneumoconiosis," caused by long-term exposure to coal dust? Black Lung Disease.

Exposure to asbestos fibers, commonly used in older construction materials, leads to which chronic lung disease? Asbestosis.

🌍 Environmental Chemistry & Energy
What is the standard unit used to measure the thickness of the ozone layer in the atmosphere? Dobson Unit (DU).

Photochemical smog, common in sunny cities with heavy traffic, contains a toxic secondary pollutant known as PAN. What does PAN stand for? Peroxyacetyl Nitrate.

Which type of smog, primarily caused by burning high-sulfur coal, is known historically as "London Smog"? Sulfurous Smog (or Peysoup Smog).

Normal rain has a pH of around 5.6. Acid rain typically has a pH below what value? 5.0.

What was the name of the highly toxic herbicide and defoliant used extensively by the U.S. military during the Vietnam War? Agent Orange.

Which specific species of lower plants are highly sensitive to sulfur dioxide pollution and act as excellent bio-indicators of air quality? Lichens.

What is the primary combustible component of Biogas (Gobar gas)? Methane.

What is the primary component of Compressed Natural Gas (CNG)? Methane.

The Bharat Stage (BS) emission standards instituted by the Government of India are based on which international regulations? The European (Euro) emission norms.

To combat severe vehicular pollution, India directly skipped which BS emission stage to jump from BS-IV to BS-VI in 2020? BS-V.

What is the fine, glass-like powdery residue generated as a byproduct by coal-fired thermal power plants? Fly Ash.

Which international organization, headquartered in Gurugram, India, was jointly launched by India and France to promote solar energy? The International Solar Alliance (ISA).

Which global initiative, launched by India in 2019, aims to promote the resilience of infrastructure systems to climate and disaster risks? Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure (CDRI).

🧪 Advanced Materials & Chemistry in Action
Which is the lightest known metal on the periodic table? Lithium.

Which is the heaviest naturally occurring element on Earth? Uranium.

Which is the most electronegative element, meaning it has the strongest tendency to attract electrons? Fluorine.

Which noble gas is commonly used in weather balloons and blimps because it is lighter than air and non-combustible? Helium.

Which radioactive noble gas can accumulate in poorly ventilated basements and poses a significant lung cancer risk? Radon.

Which remarkably strong synthetic fiber is used extensively to manufacture bulletproof vests? Kevlar.

Which was the first entirely synthetic plastic, widely used in the early 20th century for electrical insulators and telephone casings? Bakelite.

Natural rubber is fundamentally a polymer of which organic compound? Isoprene.

In chemical terms, the rusting of iron is a classic example of which type of reaction? An Oxidation reaction.

What is the primary carbohydrate (sugar) naturally present in mammalian milk? Lactose.

Which specific protein makes up the majority of proteins found in cow's milk and gives it its white color? Casein.

Which enzyme, particularly active in the stomachs of infants, is responsible for coagulating (curdling) milk proteins for digestion? Rennin.

⚙️ Everyday Physics & Mechanics
The separation of cream from milk in a dairy churner, or the spinning cycle of a washing machine, operates on which physical principle? Centrifugation (Centrifugal force).

Why do stars appear to twinkle in the night sky? Due to the atmospheric refraction of starlight as it passes through layers of different air densities.

Why do the Sun and the surrounding sky appear red during sunrise and sunset? Because red light has a longer wavelength and is scattered the least by the atmosphere compared to blue light.

What is the physical separation of white light into its constituent seven colors (a spectrum) when passing through a prism called? Dispersion.

The power of a magnifying lens is mathematically measured in which unit? Diopters.

Which electrical device is used to convert Alternating Current (AC) into Direct Current (DC)? A Rectifier.

Which electrical device is used to either increase (step-up) or decrease (step-down) the voltage of an alternating current? A Transformer.

Which specific alloy, known for its high electrical resistance and high melting point, is used as the heating element in electric irons and toasters? Nichrome.

A traditional electrical fuse wire is primarily made of an alloy of which two metals? Lead and Tin.

What are the two most crucial physical properties of a good fuse wire? High electrical resistance and a low melting point.

In which medium does sound travel the absolute fastest: solid, liquid, or gas? Solid (e.g., steel or glass).

The "pitch" of a musical sound (whether it is shrill or deep) depends strictly on which property of the sound wave? Its Frequency.

The "loudness" or volume of a sound depends on which property of the sound wave? Its Amplitude.

Bats and dolphins navigate and hunt using echolocation. What type of high-frequency sound waves do they emit? Ultrasonic waves.

What is the generally accepted audible frequency range for a healthy human ear? 20 Hertz to 20,000 Hertz.

What is the SI unit of measurement for frequency? Hertz (Hz).

What is the SI unit of measurement for pressure? Pascal (Pa).

Hydraulic brakes in cars and hydraulic lifts in garages operate fundamentally on which law of physics? Pascal's Law (pressure applied to an enclosed fluid is transmitted equally in all directions).

Which physical principle explains why a massive steel ship can float on water while a small steel nail sinks? Archimedes' Principle (Law of Buoyancy).

Archimedes principle buoyancy, AI generated
Shutterstock
⚛️ India's Nuclear Program & Milestones
Who is universally acknowledged as the founding father of the Indian nuclear program? Dr. Homi J. Bhabha.

What was the name of India's (and Asia's) very first nuclear research reactor, which went critical in 1956? Apsara.

Where is the Bhabha Atomic Research Centre (BARC) located? Trombay, Mumbai.

What was the official code name for India's first successful nuclear bomb test conducted in Pokhran in 1974? Smiling Buddha.

What was the official code name for the series of five nuclear bomb test explosions conducted by India at Pokhran in 1998? Operation Shakti.

Where is the headquarters of the Heavy Water Board, which manages the production of heavy water for India's nuclear reactors? Mumbai.

The Jaduguda mine, the first of its kind in India, is famous for the extraction of which radioactive mineral? Uranium.

India possesses some of the world's largest reserves of Thorium. Which naturally occurring coastal sand is the primary source of this Thorium? Monazite sand (found heavily along the Kerala coast).

The Kudankulam Nuclear Power Plant, currently India's largest, was built in collaboration with which country? Russia.

In which state is the Kakrapar Atomic Power Station located? Gujarat.

🛰️ Orbital Mechanics & Applied Instruments
At approximately what altitude above the Earth's equator does a satellite need to be placed to remain in a "Geostationary Orbit"? 35,786 kilometers.

What makes a geostationary orbit so crucial for communication and weather satellites? The satellite's orbital period exactly matches the Earth's rotation (24 hours), so it appears stationary over one spot on the ground.

If a spacecraft wants to break free from the gravitational pull of the Earth entirely, what precise velocity must it reach? 11.2 kilometers per second (Escape Velocity).

Which device uses a magnetic field to safely contain extremely high-temperature plasma during nuclear fusion experiments (like the ITER project)? A Tokamak.

Which instrument is widely used by meteorologists to measure the relative humidity or moisture content in the air? A Hygrometer.

If the mercury level in a barometer falls suddenly and sharply, what meteorological event does it indicate? An approaching storm or cyclone.

Which instrument is used to accurately measure wind speed and direction at meteorological stations? An Anemometer.

What is a "Galvanometer" primarily used for in electrical physics? To detect and measure very small electric currents.

What is the scientific term for the study of the origin, evolution, and eventual fate of the universe? Cosmology.

What is the study of fossils and prehistoric life called? Paleontology.

What is the specific branch of medicine that deals with the anatomy, functions, and diseases of the eye? Ophthalmology.

Which sub-discipline of botany focuses exclusively on the study of algae? Phycology.

What is the study of viral structure, function, and the diseases they cause called? Virology.

The "Richter Scale" is logarithmic. A magnitude 6.0 earthquake releases approximately how many times more energy than a 5.0 earthquake? 31.6 times more energy.

Which specific radioactive isotope is heavily utilized in medicine to treat overactive thyroid glands and thyroid cancer? Iodine-131.

Which radioactive isotope is most commonly used as a radiation source for sterilizing medical equipment and treating cancer (radiotherapy)? Cobalt-60.

What is the incredibly dense, incredibly hot state of matter that consists of a soup of positively charged ions and free electrons? Plasma (the fourth state of matter).

What is the fifth state of matter, created when a gas of bosons is cooled to temperatures very close to absolute zero? Bose-Einstein Condensate (BEC).

Absolute zero is the lowest possible temperature where fundamental particle motion completely stops. What is this temperature in Celsius? -273.15 degrees Celsius.

The Large Hadron Collider (LHC), the world's largest and most powerful particle accelerator, famously discovered the "God Particle" in 2012. What is its scientific name? The Higgs Boson.
